Two Decreasing Measures for Simply Typed λ \lambda -Terms (Extended Version)
Pablo Barenbaum, Cristian Sottile

Abstract
This paper defines two decreasing measures for terms of the simply typed λ \lambda -calculus, called the 𝒲 \mathcal{W} -measure and the 𝒯 𝐦 \mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}} -measure. A decreasing measure is a function that maps each typable λ \lambda -term to an element of a well-founded ordering, in such a way that contracting any β \beta -redex decreases the value of the function, entailing strong normalization. Both measures are defined constructively, relying on an auxiliary calculus, a non-erasing variant of the λ \lambda -calculus. In this system, dubbed the λ 𝐦 \lambda^{\mathbf{m}} -calculus, each β \beta -step creates a “wrapper” containing a copy of the argument that cannot be erased and cannot interact with the context in any other way. Both measures rely crucially on the observation, known to Turing and Prawitz, that contracting a redex cannot create redexes of higher degree, where the degree of a redex is defined as the height of the type of its λ \lambda -abstraction. The 𝒲 \mathcal{W} -measure maps each λ \lambda -term to a natural number, and it is obtained by evaluating the term in the λ 𝐦 \lambda^{\mathbf{m}} -calculus and counting the number of remaining wrappers. The 𝒯 𝐦 \mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}} -measure maps each λ \lambda -term to a structure of nested multisets, where the nesting depth is proportional to the maximum redex degree.

ICC, Universidad de Buenos Aires (CONICET), Argentina and Universidad Nacional de Quilmes, Argentinacsottile@icc.fcen.uba.ar

1
Introduction

In this paper we revisit a fundamental question,
that of
strong normalization
of the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus (STLC).
We begin by recalling
that a reduction relation is
weakly normalizing
(WN)
if every term can be reduced to normal form in a finite number
of steps,
whereas it is
strongly normalizing
(SN) if there are no infinite
reduction sequences (
a_{1}\to a_{2}\to a_{3}\to\ldots
).
Let us review three proof techniques for proving strong
normalization of the STLC.

One of the better known ways to prove that the STLC
is SN is through arguments based on
reducibility models
.
The idea is to interpret each type
A
as a set
[\![A]\!]

of strongly normalizing terms,
and to prove that each term
M
of type
A

is an element of
[\![A]\!]
.
Many variants of these ideas can be found in the literature,
including Girard’s reducibility candidates
[
17
]

and Tait’s saturated sets
[
30
]
.
These techniques provide relatively succint proofs and
they generalize well to extensions of the STLC,

e.g.
to dependent type theory
[
6
]
or
classical calculi
[
13
]
.
On the other hand, the abstract nature of reducibility arguments
does not provide a “tangible” insight
on why a
\beta
-reduction step brings a term closer to normal form.
More specifically, reducibility arguments do not construct explicit

decreasing measures
. By decreasing measure we mean a function
“
\#
” mapping each
\lambda
-term to a well-founded ordering
(X,>)

such that
M\to_{\beta}N
implies
\#(M)>\#(N)
.

Another way to prove strong normalization is based on
redex degrees
.
A
redex
in the STLC is an applied abstraction,

i.e.
a term of the form
(\lambda x.\,M)\,N
.
The
degree
of a redex is defined as the
height

of the type of its abstraction.
A crucial observation,
that can be attributed to an unpublished note of Turing
(as reported by Gandy
[
15
]
; see also
[
4
]
),
is that
contracting a redex cannot create a redex of higher or equal degree
.
Recall that a redex
S
is
created
by the contraction of a redex
R

if
S
has no
ancestor
before
R
.
Indeed, as shown by Lévy
[
22
]
, in the
\lambda
-calculus,
redexes can be created in exactly one of the three ways below:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsf{{{1}}}}&(\underline{\lambda}x.\,x)\,(\lambda y.\,M)\,N&\to_{\beta}&(\underline{\lambda}y.\,M)\,N\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsf{{{2}}}}&(\underline{\lambda}x.\,\lambda y.\,M)\,N\,P&\to_{\beta}&(\underline{\lambda}y.\,M[x:=N])\,P\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsf{{{3}}}}&(\underline{\lambda}x.\,\ldots x\,M\ldots)\,(\lambda y.\,N)&\to_{\beta}&\ldots(\underline{\lambda}y.\,N)\,M[x:=\lambda y.\,N]\ldots\end{array}

where we underline the
\lambda
of the contracted redex on the left,
and the
\lambda
of the created redex on the right.
In each of these cases, it can be seen that the degree of the created
redex is strictly lower than the degree of the contracted redex.
For instance, in creation case
1
,
the type of the contracted redex
is of the form
(A\to B)\to(A\to B)
,
while the type of the created redex is
A\to B
,
so the height strictly decreases.

With this fact in mind, for each term
M

one can define
what we call
Turing’s measure
,

i.e.
the multiset
\mathcal{T}(M)
of the degrees
of all the redexes of
M
.
One may hope that any reduction step
M\to_{\beta}N

decreases the measure,
i.e.

\mathcal{T}(M)\succ\mathcal{T}(N)
,
where “
\succ
” is the usual
well-founded multiset ordering
induced by the ordering
(\mathbb{N},>)
of its elements
[
12
]
.
Unfortunately, this is not the case:
even though contracting a redex can only create redexes of strictly lower
degree, it can still make an arbitrary number of
copies

of redexes of arbitrarily large degrees.

In his notes, Turing observed that one can follow a reduction strategy
that always selects the
rightmost
redex of highest degree.
This strategy ensures that the contracted redex does not copy redexes
of higher or
equal degree, which makes the
\mathcal{T}(-)
measure strictly decrease,
thus proving that the
\lambda
-calculus is WN.
An even simpler measure that also decreases, if one follows this strategy, is

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(M)=(D,n)
, where
D
is the maximum degree of the redexes in
M

and
n
is the number of redexes of degree
D
in
M
.
Similar ideas were exploited by Prawitz
[
28
]

and Gentzen (as reported by von Plato
[
27
]
)
to normalize proofs in natural deduction.
After WN has been established, an indirect proof of SN can be obtained
by translating each typable
\lambda
-term
M
to
a typable term
M^{\prime}
of the
\lambda{I}
-calculus
;
see for instance
[
29
, Section 3.5]
.

In summary, redex degrees can be used to define
concrete measures such as
\mathcal{T}(M)

and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(M)
,
that are computable in linear time
and decrease when following a particular reduction strategy.
As already mentioned,
these measures do not necessarily decrease when contracting arbitrary
\beta
-redexes.

A third way to prove SN relies
on an interpretation that maps terms to
increasing functionals
.
This approach was pioneered by Gandy
[
16
]

and refined by de Vrijer
[
10
]
.
Each type
A
is mapped to a partially ordered set
[\![A]\!]
.
Specifically, base types are mapped to
(\mathbb{N},\leq)
,
and
[\![A\to B]\!]
is defined as the set of
strictly increasing functions
[\![A]\!]\to[\![B]\!]
,
partially ordered by the point-wise order.
Each term
M
of type
A
is interpreted as
an element
[M]\in[\![A]\!]
.
Moreover, an element
f\in[\![A]\!]

can be projected to a natural number
f\star\in\mathbb{N}

in such a way that
M\to_{\beta}N

implies
[M]\star>[N]\star
.
This indeed provides a decreasing measure.
One of the downsides of this measure
is that computing
[M]\star

is essentially as difficult as evaluating
M
,
because
[M]
is defined as a higher-order functional
with a similar structure as the
\lambda
-term
M
itself.

In this work we propose
two decreasing measures for the STLC
,
that we dub the
\mathcal{W}
-measure and the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure,
and we prove that they are decreasing.
An ideal decreasing measure should fulfill multiple (partly subjective)
requirements:

1.

the measure should be easy to calculate,
in terms of computational complexity;

2.

its codomain (a well-founded ordering)
should be simple, in terms of its ordinal type;

3.

it should give us insight on why
\beta
-reduction terminates;

4.

it should be easy to prove that the measure is decreasing.
A measure that excels simultaneously at all these requirements is elusive,
and perhaps unattainable.
The proposed measures have different strengths and weaknesses.

Contributions and structure of this document

The
\mathcal{W}
-measure and the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure are defined by means
of on an auxiliary calculus that we dub the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus.
The remainder of the paper is structured as follows.

In
Section
2

we
define the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus
.
It is an extension of the STLC with terms
1
1

1

Note that terms of the

\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus are ranged over by
t,s,\ldots

(rather than
M,N,\ldots
).

of the form
t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}
,
called
wrappers
.
A wrapper
t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}
should be understood as
essentially the term
t

in which
s
is a
memorized term
, that is, leftover garbage that can
be reduced but cannot interact with the context in any way.
The type of
t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}
is the same as the type of
t
,
disregarding the type of
s
.

The
\beta
-reduction rule is modified so that
contracting a redex
(\lambda x.\,t)\,s
,
besides substituting the free occurrences of
x
by
s
in
t
,
produces a wrapper that contains a copy of the argument
s
.
The reduction rule is

(\lambda x.\,t)\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\ldots\bm{\{}u_{n}\bm{\}}\,s\,\,\to_{\mathbf{m}}\,\,t[x:=s]\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\ldots\bm{\{}u_{n}\bm{\}}
.
Note that we allow the presence of an arbitrary number of memorized terms
mediating between the abstraction and the application.
This is to avoid memorized terms
blocking
redexes.
For example, if
I=\lambda x.\,x
:

(\lambda x.\,x(xy))I\to_{\mathbf{m}}(I(Iy))\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}(Iy)\bm{\{}Iy\bm{\}}\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}(Iy)\bm{\{}y\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}y\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\{}y\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}

Then we study some syntactic properties of
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
.
In particular, we define a relation
t\mathrel{\rhd}s

of
forgetful reduction
, meaning that
s
is obtained from
t

by erasing one memorized subterm.
For example,

x\,\bm{\{}x\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\{}y\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}x\,\bm{\{}y\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\bm{\}}
.
Forgetful reduction is used as a technical tool to prove that the measures
are decreasing in the following sections.

In
Section
3
,
we
propose the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
(Def.
12
),
and we prove that it is decreasing.
To define the
\mathcal{W}
-measure,
we resort to an operation
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)

that simultaneously
contracts all the redexes of degree
d
in a term of the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus,
that is, the result of the
complete development

of all the redexes of degree
d
.
The degree of a redex

(\lambda x.\,t)\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\ldots\bm{\{}u_{n}\bm{\}}\,s

is defined similarly as for the STLC,
as the height of the type of the abstraction.
To calculate the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
of a
\lambda
-term
M
,
let
D
be the maximum degree of the redexes in
M
,
and define
\mathcal{W}(M)

as the number of wrappers in
\mathtt{S}_{1}(\mathtt{S}_{2}(\ldots\mathtt{S}_{D}(M)))
.
For example, if

M=(\lambda x.\,x\,(x\,y))\,(\lambda z.\,w)
,
it turns out that

\mathtt{S}_{1}(\mathtt{S}_{2}(M))=w\bm{\{}w\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\{}\lambda z.\,w\bm{\}}

which has three wrappers,
so
\mathcal{W}(M)=3
.
The
\mathcal{W}
-measure maps each typable
\lambda
-term
to a natural number.
The main result of Section
3

is Thm.
15
,
stating that
\mathcal{W}
is decreasing
,

i.e.
that
M\to_{\beta}N
implies
\mathcal{W}(M)>\mathcal{W}(N)
.

In
Section
4
we study
reduction by degrees
,
a restricted notion of reduction in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus,
written
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s
,
meaning that
t
reduces to
s
by contracting a redex of degree
d
.
This section contains technical commutation, termination, and postponement results.

In
Section
5
,
we
propose the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure
, and we prove that it is decreasing.
To define the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure,
we define two auxiliary measures
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)
and
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{D}(t)
,
indexed by a natural number
D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, mutually recursively:

•

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)

is the multiset of pairs
(d,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t))
,
for each redex occurrence of degree
d\leq D
in
t
;

•

\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{D}(t)

is the multiset of elements
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D-1}(t^{\prime})
,
for each reduction sequence
t\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.

The measure
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)
is defined for every
D\geq 0
,
while
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{D}(t)
is defined only for
D\geq 1
.
Multisets are ordered according to the usual multiset ordering,
and pairs according to the lexicographic ordering.
To calculate the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure of a
\lambda
-term
M
,
let
D
be the maximum degree of the redexes in
M
,
and define
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq}(M)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(M)
.
The measure
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq}(M)
yields a structure of
nested multisets of nesting depth at most
2D
.
The main theorem of Section
3

is Thm.
32
,
stating that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
is decreasing
,

i.e.
that
M\to_{\beta}N
implies
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq}(M)>\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq}(N)
.

Finally, in
Section
6
, we conclude.

2
The
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus

As mentioned in the introduction, the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus is an extension
of the STLC in which the
\beta
-reduction rule keeps an extra memorized
copy of the argument in a “wrapper”
t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}
,
in such a way that contracting a redex
like
(\lambda x.\,t)\,s
does not erase
s
,
even if
x
does not occur free in
t
.
In this section we define the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus and we prove some
of the properties that are needed in the following sections
to prove that the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
and the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure are decreasing.
In particular, we discuss
subject reduction
(Prop.
3
)
and
confluence
(Prop.
4
);
we define an operation of
simplification
(Def.
5
)
which turns out to calculate
the normal form of a term (Prop.
7
);
and we define the relation called
forgetful reduction
(Def.
8
),
which is shown to commute with reduction (Prop.
10
).

First we fix the notation and nomenclature.

Types
of the STLC are either
base types (
\alpha,\beta,\ldots
) or arrow types (
A\to B
).

Terms
are either variables (
x^{A},y^{A},\ldots
),
abstractions (
\lambda x^{A}.\,M
),
or applications (
M\,N
), with the usual typing rules.
Terms are defined up to
\alpha
-renaming of bound variables.
We adopt an
à la
Church presentation of the STLC,
but we omit most type decorations on variables as long as there is little
danger of confusion.
The
\beta
-reduction rule is

(\lambda x.\,M)\,N\to_{\beta}M[x:=N]

where
M[x:=N]
is the capture-avoiding substitution
of the free occurrences of
x
in
M
by
N
.

The
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus: syntax and reduction

The set of
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-terms
—or just
terms
—
is given by

t,s,\ldots::=x^{A}\mid\lambda x^{A}.\,t\mid t\,s\mid t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}
.
The four kinds of terms are respectively called

variables
,
abstractions
,
applications
,
and
wrappers
.
In a wrapper
t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}
,
the subterm
t
is called the
body

and
s
is called the
memorized term
.
As in the STLC, we usually omit type annotations
and terms are regarded up to
\alpha
-renaming.
A
context
is a term
\mathtt{C}
with a single free
occurrence of a distinguished variable
\Box
,
and
\mathtt{C}[t]
is the variable-capturing substitution
of the occurrence of
\Box
in
\mathtt{C}
by
t
.

Typing judgments
are of the form
\Gamma\vdash t:A

where
\Gamma
is a partial function mapping variables to types.
Derivable typing judgments are defined by the following rules:

{\small{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\\
&\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!\Gamma,x:A\vdash x^{A}:A\end{array}}\hskip 14.22636pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!\Gamma,x:A\vdash t:B\\
&\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!\Gamma\vdash\lambda x^{A}.\,t:A\to B\end{array}}\hskip 14.22636pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!\Gamma\vdash t:A\to B\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash s:A\\
&\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!\Gamma\vdash t\,s:B\end{array}}\hskip 14.22636pt{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!\Gamma\vdash t:A\hskip 14.22636pt\Gamma\vdash s:B\\
&\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!\Gamma\vdash t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}:A\end{array}}}

A term
t
is
typable
if
\Gamma\vdash t:A
holds
for some
\Gamma
and some
A
. Unless otherwise specified, when we
speak of “terms” we mean “typable terms”.
It is straightforward to show that a typable term has a unique type.
We write
\mathsf{type}(t)
for the type of
t
.

A
memory
, written
\mathtt{L}
, is a list of memorized terms,
given by the grammar

\mathtt{L}::=\Box\mid\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}
.
If
t
is a term and
\mathtt{L}
is a memory, we write

t\mathtt{L}
for the term that results from appending all the memorized terms
in
\mathtt{L}
to
t
, that is,

(t)(\Box\bm{\{}s_{1}\bm{\}}\ldots\bm{\{}s_{n}\bm{\}})=t\bm{\{}s_{1}\bm{\}}\ldots\bm{\{}s_{n}\bm{\}}
.
We write
t[x:=s]

for the operation of capture-avoiding substitution of the free occurrences
of
x
in
t
by
s
.
The
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus is the rewriting system whose objects
are typable
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-terms, endowed with the following
notion of reduction, closed by compatibility under arbitrary contexts:

Definition 1

(Reduction in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus)
.

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s\,\,\to_{\mathbf{m}}\,\,t[x:=s]\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}

Abstractions followed by lists of memorized terms,

i.e.
terms of the form
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}
,
are called
\mathbf{m}
-abstractions
.
Note that all abstractions are also
\mathbf{m}
-abstractions, as
\mathtt{L}
may
be empty.
A
redex
is an expression matching the left-hand side of the

\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-reduction rule, which must be an
applied
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
,

i.e.
a term of the form

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s
.
The
height
of a type is given by

0pt{\alpha}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}0

and

0pt{A\to B}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}1+\max(0pt{A},0pt{B})
.
The
degree
of a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}

is defined as the height of its type;
note that this number is always strictly positive, since the type
must be of the form
A\to B
.
Moreover, this type is unique, so the operation is well-defined.
The
degree
of a redex

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s

is defined as the degree of the
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}
.
The
max-degree
of a term
t

is written
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)

and it is defined as the maximum degree of the redexes in
t
,
or
0
if
t
has no redexes.
The
weight

\mathsf{w}(t)
of a
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-term
t

is the number of wrappers in
t
.

Example 2
.

Let
0
be a base type
and let

t:=(\lambda x^{0\to 0}.\,\lambda y^{0}.\,y^{0}\bm{\{}x^{0\to 0}\,(x^{0\to 0}\,z^{0})\bm{\}})\,I\,w^{0}
,
where
I:=\lambda x^{0}.\,x^{0}
.
One possible way to reduce
t
is:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,\,\,}l@{\,\,\,\,}l@{\,\,\,\,}l@{\,\,\,\,}l}(\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,y\bm{\{}x\,(x\,z)\bm{\}})\,I\,w&\to_{\mathbf{m}}&(\lambda y.\,y\bm{\{}I\,(I\,z)\bm{\}})\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}\,w&\to_{\mathbf{m}}&w\bm{\{}I\,(I\,z)\bm{\}}\bm{\{}w\bm{\}}\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}\\
&\to_{\mathbf{m}}&w\bm{\{}I\,(z\bm{\{}z\bm{\}})\bm{\}}\,\bm{\{}w\bm{\}}\,\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}&\to_{\mathbf{m}}&w\bm{\{}z\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\bm{\{}z\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\{}w\bm{\}}\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}=s\end{array}

The degrees of the redexes contracted in each step are
2
,
1
,
1
, and
1
,
in that order. Note that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)=2
and that the weight of the
resulting term is
\mathsf{w}(s)=6
.

Two basic properties of the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus are
subject reduction and confluence.
These are immediate consequences of the fact that the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus
can be understood as an
orthogonal HRS
in the sense of
Nipkow
[
26
]
,

i.e.
a left-linear higher-order rewriting system without critical pairs.

Proposition 3

(Subject reduction)
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:A
and
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
.
Then
\Gamma\vdash s:A
.

Proposition 4

(Confluence)
.

If
t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{2}
and
t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{3}
,
there exists a term
t_{4}
such that

t_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{4}
and
t_{3}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{4}
.

Full simplification

Next, we define an operation written
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)

and called
full simplification
.

Let
d\geq 1
be a natural number.
The
simplification of degree
d
, written
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
,
is the result of simultaneously contracting
all the redexes of degree
d
in
t
,
that is,
the result of the
complete development
of all redexes of degree
d
.
Formally, for each
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-term
t

we define
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
,
and, for each memory
\mathtt{L}
, we define
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})

as follows:

Definition 5

(Simplification)
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\mathtt{S}_{d}(x)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&x\\
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\lambda x.\,t)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\lambda x.\,\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)\\
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t\,s)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\begin{cases}\mathtt{S}_{d}(t^{\prime})[x:=\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)]\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)\bm{\}}\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})&\text{if $t=(\lambda x.\,t^{\prime})\mathtt{L}$ and it is of degree $d$}\\
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)\,\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)\bm{\}}\end{array}

where if
\mathtt{L}
is a memory,
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})

is defined by
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\Box)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Box

and
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)\bm{\}}
.
Furthermore, if
t
is a
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-term of max-degree
D
, we define
the
full simplification
of
t
as the term that results from
iteratively taking the simplification of degree
i
from
D
down to
1
.
More precisely,

\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathtt{S}_{1}(\ldots\mathtt{S}_{D-1}(\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)))
.

Example 6
.

Consider the
\lambda
-term

M=(\lambda x^{0\to 0}.\,x^{0\to 0}(x^{0\to 0}\,y^{0}))(\lambda z^{0}.\,w^{0})
.
It can be regarded also as a
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-term, and we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathtt{S}_{2}(M)&=&((\lambda z^{0}.\,w^{0})\,((\lambda z^{0}.\,w^{0})\,y^{0}))\bm{\{}\lambda z^{0}.\,w^{0}\bm{\}}\\
\mathtt{S}_{*}(M)=\mathtt{S}_{1}(\mathtt{S}_{2}(M))&=&w^{0}\bm{\{}w^{0}\bm{\{}y^{0}\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\{}\lambda z^{0}.\,w^{0}\bm{\}}\end{array}

Note that
M
has only one redex,
whose abstraction is of type
(0\to 0)\to 0

and hence of degree
2
,
and that
\mathtt{S}_{2}(M)

has two redexes, whose abstractions are of type
0\to 0

and hence of degree
1
.
Moreover, consider the
\lambda
-term

N=(\lambda z^{0}.\,w^{0})\,((\lambda z^{0}.\,w^{0})\,y^{0})
. Then

\mathtt{S}_{*}(N)=\mathtt{S}_{1}(N)=w\bm{\{}w\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\}}
.
Note that
N

has two redexes whose abstraction is of type
0\to 0

and hence of degree
1
.
As an additional note, in the
\lambda
-calculus
there is a reduction step
M\to_{\beta}N
,
and we have that

\mathsf{w}(\mathtt{S}_{*}(M))=3>2=\mathsf{w}(\mathtt{S}_{*}(N))
.
So this example illustrates that the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
(as defined in Def.
12
) is decreasing
(as we will show in Thm.
15
).

As it turns out,
full simplification corresponds
to reduction to normal form
. More precisely, we have the
following result, which entails in particular that the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus
is weakly normalizing:

Proposition 7
.

t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
,
and moreover
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

Proof.

To show that
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
, it suffices to prove a lemma
stating that
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
for all
d\geq 1
.
This implies that

t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{D-1}(\mathtt{S}_{D}(t))\ldots\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{1}(\ldots\mathtt{S}_{D-1}(\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)))=\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
,
where
D
is the max-degree of
t
.
The lemma itself is straightforward by induction on
t
.

To show that
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
the key property is that, after performing a simplification of order
d
,
no redexes of order
d
remain.
The reason is that contracting a redex of order
d
can only
create redexes of lower degree.
More precisely, we prove a key lemma stating that
if
d\geq 1
and
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)\leq d
,
then
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{d}(t))<d
.
If we let
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)\leq D
,
we can iterate this lemma, to obtain
that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{D}(t))<D
,
and
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{D-1}(\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)))<D-1
,

\ldots
,
and finally

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{1}(\ldots\mathtt{S}_{D-1}(\mathtt{S}_{D}(t))))<1
.
This means that
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{1}(\ldots\mathtt{S}_{D-1}(\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)))

does not contain redexes, since there are no redexes of degree
0
,
so
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
must be a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

See Prop.
43
in the appendix for detailed proofs.

∎

Forgetful reduction

To conclude this section, we introduce the relation of
forgetful reduction

t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
, and we prove that it commutes with reduction.

Definition 8
.

A
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-term
t

reduces via a forgetful step to
s
,
written
t\mathrel{\rhd}s
,
according to the following axiom,
closed by compatibility under arbitrary contexts:

t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}t

We say that
t
reduces via forgetful reduction to
s

if and only if
t\mathrel{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}s
,
where
\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}
denotes the transitive closure of
\mathrel{\rhd}
.

Example 9
.

(\lambda x.\,x\bm{\{}y\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\}})\bm{\{}z\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}(\lambda x.\,x\bm{\{}y\bm{\{}y\bm{\}}\bm{\}})\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}(\lambda x.\,x)\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}\lambda x.\,x
.

Proposition 10

(Forgetful reduction commutes with reduction)
.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
and
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
s^{\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s^{\prime}
and
s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}s^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,
if
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
and
t
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
then
s
is also a normal form.

Proof.

The result can be reduced to a local commutation result,
stating that if
t\mathrel{\rhd}s
and
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
s^{\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s^{\prime}
and
s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{=}s^{\prime}
,
where
\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{=}
is the reflexive closure of
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
.
Local commutation can be proved by case analysis.
The interesting cases are when a shrinking step
s\mathrel{\rhd}s^{\prime}

lies inside the argument of a redex,
and when a reduction step
r\to_{\mathbf{m}}r^{\prime}
is inside erased garbage:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t[x:=s]\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{t[x:=s^{\prime}]\bm{\{}s^{\prime}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}}

\textstyle{u\bm{\{}r\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{u\bm{\{}r^{\prime}\bm{\}}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{u}

For the last part of the statement,
it suffices to show that
if
t\mathrel{\rhd}s
in one step and
t
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
then
s
is also a normal form,
which is straightforward by induction on
t
.

See Prop.
46
in the appendix
for detailed proofs.

∎

Each step in the STLC has a
corresponding

step in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus, that contracts the redex in the
same position.
For instance the step
(\lambda x.\,x\,y)\,I\to_{\beta}I\,y

in the STLC
has a corresponding step
(\lambda x.\,x\,y)\,I\to_{\mathbf{m}}(I\,y)\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}

in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus.
In this example,
(I\,y)\bm{\{}I\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}I\,y
.
The following easy lemma confirms that this is a general fact:

Lemma 11

(Reduce/forget lemma)
.

Let
M\to_{\beta}N
be a
\beta
-step,
and let
M\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
be the corresponding step in
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
.
Then
s\mathrel{\rhd}N
.

3
The
\mathcal{W}
-measure

In this section, we
define the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
(Def.
12
)
and we
prove that it is decreasing
(Thm.
15
).
Let us try to convey some ideas that led to the definition of the

\mathcal{W}
-measure.
Recall that an abstract rewriting system
(A,\to)

is
weakly Church–Rosser
(WCR) if

\leftarrow\rightarrow\,\subseteq\,\rightarrow^{*}\leftarrow^{*}
,

Church–Rosser
(CR) if

\leftarrow^{*}\rightarrow^{*}\,\subseteq\,\rightarrow^{*}\leftarrow^{*}
,
and
increasing
(Inc)
if there exists a function
|\cdot|:A\to\mathbb{N}
such that

a\to b
implies
|a|<|b|
.
Let us also recall

Klop–Nederpelt’s lemma

[
31
, Theorem 1.2.3 (iii)]
,
which states that Inc
\land
WCR
\land
WN
\implies
SN
\land
CR.

Let
(A,\to)
be increasing and WCR.
Given a reduction
a\to^{*}b
, where
b
is a normal form,
we can find a
decreasing
measure for
the set of objects reachable from
a
, that is,
the set
\{c\in A\ |\ a\to^{*}c\}
.
In fact, by Klop–Nederpelt’s lemma,
we know that for every
c\in A
such that
a\to^{*}c

we have that
c\to^{*}b
,
which implies that
|c|\leq|b|
,
and hence we can define
\#(c):=|b|-|c|
.
It is easy to see that
\#(-)
is a decreasing measure,
since
c\to c^{\prime}
implies that
|c|<|c^{\prime}|

so
\#(c):=|b|-|c|>|b|-|c^{\prime}|=\#(c^{\prime})
.
Furthermore, the value of
\#(c)
does not depend on the choice
of
a
, by uniqueness of normal forms.

The idea behind the
\mathcal{W}
-measure is that
the construction of a
decreasing
measure
can be based on an
increasing
measure,
according to the previous observation.
It is not possible to build an increasing measure directly
for the STLC;
e.g.
the following
infinite sequence of expansions

t\leftarrow I\,t\leftarrow I\,(I\,t)\leftarrow\ldots

would induce an infinite decreasing chain of natural numbers

|t|>|I\,t|>|I\,(I\,t)|>\ldots
.

One could try to define an increasing measure
in a variant of the STLC
such as Endrullis
et al.
’s clocked
\lambda
-calculus
[
14
]
,
in which
the
\beta
-rule becomes

(\lambda x.\,t)\,s\to{\tau}(t[x:=s])
,
that is, contracting a
\beta
-redex produces a counter “
{\tau}
” that
keeps track of the number of contracted redexes.
One could then count the number of
\tau
’s:
for example, in the reduction sequence

(\lambda x.\,x\,(x\,y))\,I\to{\tau}(I\,(I\,y))\to{\tau}{\tau}(I\,y)\to{\tau}{\tau}{\tau}y

the number of counters strictly increases with each step.
Unfortunately, this does not define an increasing measure, due to
erasure
.
For example,
(\lambda x.\,y)\,t\to{\tau}y

erases all the counters in
t
.

This is the motivation behind the definition of the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus,
which avoids erasure by always keeping an extra copy of the argument in a wrapper.
The
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus is indeed increasing: in a step
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}s

one has that
\mathsf{w}(t)<\mathsf{w}(s)
,
where we recall that
\mathsf{w}(t)
denotes the
weight
,

i.e.
the number of wrappers in
t
.
For example, the step

(\lambda x.\,y)\,(z\bm{\{}z\bm{\}})\to_{\mathbf{m}}y\bm{\{}z\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\bm{\}}

increases the number of wrappers.
The decreasing measure
\mathcal{W}(M)
is defined essentially
by reducing
M
to normal form in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus
and counting the number of wrappers in the result:

Definition 12

(The
\mathcal{W}
-measure)
.

For each typable
\lambda
-term
M
,
define
\mathcal{W}(M)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathsf{w}(\mathtt{S}_{*}(M))
.

As we show below,
\mathtt{S}_{*}(M)
turns out to be
exactly the normal form of
M
in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus.
We insist in writing
\mathtt{S}_{*}(M)

to emphasize that the
definition
of the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
does not require to prove that the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus is weakly normalizing.
Indeed, the simplification
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)

can be defined by structural induction on
t
,
and the full simplification

\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{1}(\mathtt{S}_{2}(\ldots\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)))

can be calculated in exactly
D
iterations.
On the other hand, the
proof
that the
\mathcal{W}
-measure is
decreasing does rely on the fact that

\mathtt{S}_{*}(M)
is the normal form of
M
.

In the remainder of this section, we prove that the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
is indeed decreasing.
The following lemma states that forgetful reduction decreases weight,
and it is straightforward to prove:

Lemma 13
.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
then
\mathsf{w}(t)>\mathsf{w}(s)
.

The proof that the
\mathcal{W}
-measure decreases relies on the two following
properties that relate full simplification
\mathtt{S}_{*}(-)

respectively with reduction (
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
) and forgetful reduction (
\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}
):

Lemma 14
.

1.

If
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
then
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
.

2.

If
t\rhd^{+}s
then
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)\rhd^{+}\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
.

Proof.

For the first item,
note that by Prop.
7
,
we know that

t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)

and that
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
,
where moreover
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
and
\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)

are
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal forms.
By confluence (Prop.
4
), this means that

\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
.

For the second item,
note that by Prop.
7
,
we know that

t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
.
Since we also know
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
by hypothesis,
and since forgetful reduction commutes with reduction (Prop.
10
),
there exists a term
u

such that
s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}u
and
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}u
.
By Prop.
7
we know that

\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
is in normal form, so by Prop.
10

u
must also be a normal form.
On the other hand,
by Prop.
7
we know that

s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
,
where
\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
must also be a normal form.
In summary, we have that

s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}u

and

s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
,
where both
u
and
\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)

are normal forms.
By confluence (Prop.
4
)
u=\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
,
and from this we obtain that

\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}u=\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)
,
as required.
∎

Theorem 15
.

Let
M,N
be typable
\lambda
-terms
such that
M\to_{\beta}N
.
Then
\mathcal{W}(M)>\mathcal{W}(N)
.

Proof.

Given the step
M\to_{\beta}N
,
consider the corresponding step
M\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
,
and note that
s\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}N
by
the reduce/forget lemma (Lem.
11
).
Since
M\to_{\mathbf{m}}s\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}N
,
by Lem.
14
,
we have that

\mathtt{S}_{*}(M)=\mathtt{S}_{*}(s)\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}\mathtt{S}_{*}(N)
.
Finally, by Lem.
13
,

\mathcal{W}(M)=\mathsf{w}(\mathtt{S}_{*}(M))>\mathsf{w}(\mathtt{S}_{*}(N))=\mathcal{W}(N)
.
∎

The following is one example that the
\mathcal{W}
-measure decreases
(see Ex.
6
for another example):

Example 16
.

Let

M=(\lambda x^{0}.\,y^{0\to 0\to 0}\,x^{0}\,x^{0})\,((\lambda x^{0\to 0}.\,x^{0\to 0}\,z^{0})\,f^{0\to 0})
,
consider the step

M=(\lambda x.\,y\,x\,x)((\lambda x.\,x\,z)\,f)\to_{\beta}(\lambda x.\,y\,x\,x)\,(f\,z)=N
, and note that

\mathcal{W}(M)=\mathsf{w}(\mathtt{S}_{*}(M))=4>1=\mathcal{W}(N)
,
since:

\mathtt{S}_{*}(M)=(y\,(f\,z)\bm{\{}f\bm{\}}\,(f\,z)\bm{\{}f\bm{\}})\bm{\{}(f\,z)\bm{\{}f\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{S}_{*}(N)=(y\,(f\,z)\,(f\,z))\bm{\{}f\,z\bm{\}}

4
Reduction by degrees

This section is of purely technical nature.
The aim is to develop tools
that we use in the following section to reason about the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure.
To do so, we need to introduce witnesses of steps and
reduction sequences, treating the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus as
an
abstract rewriting system
in the sense of
[
31
, Def. 8.2.2]

or as a
transition system
in the sense of
[
24
, Def. 1]
.

Objects
are
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-terms,

steps
are 5-uples
R=(\mathtt{C},x,t,\mathtt{L},s)

witnessing the reduction step

\mathtt{C}[(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s]\to_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{C}[t[x:=s]\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}]

under a context
\mathtt{C}
, and

reductions
(
\rho,\sigma,\ldots
)
are sequences of composable steps.
Similarly,
forgetful steps
are triples
R=(\mathtt{C},t,s)

witnessing the forgetful reduction
\mathtt{C}[t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}]\mathrel{\rhd}\mathtt{C}[t]
,
and
forgetful reductions
(also written
\rho,\sigma,\ldots
)
are sequences of composable forgetful steps.
We write
\rho^{\mathsf{src}}
and
\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}
respectively for the
source and target terms of
\rho
.

For each
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
we define
reduction of degree
d
as follows:

Definition 17
.

t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s

if and only if

t\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
by contracting a redex of degree
d
.

We write
R:t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s

if
R
is a step witnessing a reduction step of degree
d
,
and
\rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s

if
\rho
is a reduction witnessing a sequence of
reduction steps of degree
d
.

The following results require to explicitly manipulate steps and reductions.

We only give sketches of the proofs for lack of space.
See Section
A.2
in the appendix for
detailed proofs.

Proposition 18

(Commutation of reduction by degrees)
.

For any two reductions

\rho:t_{1}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{2}

and
\sigma:t_{1}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{3}
,
there exists a term
t_{4}

and one can construct
reductions

\sigma/\rho:t_{2}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}

and
\rho/\sigma:t_{3}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}

such that,
furthermore, if
d\neq D
, then

1.

\rho/\sigma

contains at least as many steps as
\rho
;
and

2.

\rho/\sigma
determines
\rho
,
that is,

\rho_{1}/\sigma=\rho_{2}/\sigma

implies
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
.

Proof.

This is reduced to the fact that the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus can be understood
as an orthogonal higher-order rewriting system in the sense of Nipkow
[
26
]
.
Indeed,
\rho/\sigma
and
\sigma/\rho
can be taken to be
the standard notion of projection based on residuals for orthogonal HRSs.
Note that
item
1.
holds because the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus is non-erasing
while
item
2.
is a consequence of the
unique ancestor
property,

i.e.
each redex
descends
from at most one redex.
∎

Corollary 19

(Termination of reduction by degrees)
.

The relation
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
is strongly normalizing.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the fact that HRSs enjoy the
Finite Developments property
[
31
, Theorem 11.5.11]
,
observing that reduction of degree
d
does not create redexes of degree
d
.
Alternatively, it can be easily shown that
t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)

and
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
so
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
is WN.
Moreover, one can observe that
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
is

uniformly normalizing

[
19
]
,
given that there is no erasure, which entails that
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
is SN.
∎

Proposition 20

(Lifting property for lower steps)
.

Let
d<D
and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}
.
Then there exist terms
t^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}

such that
t\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

and
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}

and
t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

Note that
t\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)
.
By Prop.
18
,
there exists a term
u
such that

s\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u

and

\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u
.
Again by Prop.
18
,
there exists
s^{\prime\prime}

such that
u\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}

and
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}
.
Moreover,
\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)
is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
Since
\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u
with
d<D

and reduction does not create redexes of higher degree,

u
is also in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
so
u=s^{\prime\prime}
, and we are done.
∎

Proposition 21

(Postponement of forgetful reduction)
.

For any two reductions

\rho:t\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}t^{\prime}

and
\sigma:t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
s

and reductions
\rho^{\curvearrowright}\sigma:s\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}s^{\prime}

and
\sigma^{\curvearrowleft}\rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s
.
Furthermore,
\sigma^{\curvearrowleft}\rho
determines
\sigma
,
that is,

\sigma_{1}^{\curvearrowleft}\rho=\sigma_{2}^{\curvearrowleft}\rho

implies
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
.

Proof.

This can be reduced to an analysis of the critical pairs
between the rewriting rules defining
\mathrel{\rhd}^{-1}
and
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
.
Critical pairs are of the form

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}_{1}\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{2}\,u\mathrel{\rhd}(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}_{1}\mathtt{L}_{2}\,u\to_{\mathbf{m}}t[x:=u]\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{1}\mathtt{L}_{2}

and can be closed by

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}_{1}\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{2}\,u\to_{\mathbf{m}}t[x:=u]\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{1}\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{2}\mathrel{\rhd}t[x:=u]\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{1}\mathtt{L}_{2}
.
∎

The following diagrams depict the statements of the three preceding propositions:

18

\scriptstyle{\sigma/\rho}

\scriptstyle{\rho/\sigma}
\scriptstyle{d}

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

5
The
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure

In this section, we
define the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure
(Def.
25
)
and
we
prove that it is decreasing
(Thm.
32
).
We start with some preliminary notions.

A partially ordered set
(X,>)
is
well-founded

if there are no infinite decreasing
chains.
\mathbb{M}(X)
denotes the set of
finite multisets
over a set
X
,
which are functions

\mathfrak{m}:X\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
\mathfrak{m}(x)>0
for finitely many values of
x\in X
.
We write
\mathfrak{m}+\mathfrak{n}
for the sum of multisets,
and
x\in\mathfrak{m}
if
\mathfrak{m}(x)>0
.
We write
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
for the multiset of elements

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, taking multiplicities into account.
If
X
is a finite set and
f:X\to Y
is a function,
we use the “multiset builder” notation

[f(x)\ ||\ x\in X]
to denote the multiset

\sum_{x\in X}[f(x)]
.
If
(X,>)
is a partially ordered set,
we define a binary relation
\succ^{1}
on multisets
by declaring that

\mathfrak{m}+[x]\mathrel{\succ^{1}}\mathfrak{m}+\mathfrak{n}

if
x>y
for every
y\in\mathfrak{n}
.
The
multiset order
induced by
(X,>)

is the strict order relation on multisets
defined by declaring that

\mathfrak{m}\succ\mathfrak{n}
if and only if
\mathfrak{m}\mathrel{(\mathrel{\succ^{1}})^{+}}\mathfrak{n}
.
We recall the following widely known theorem
by Dershowitz and Manna
[
12
]
:

Theorem 22
.

If
(X,>)
is well-founded,
then
(\mathbb{M}(X),\succ)
is well-founded.

As usual,
\mathfrak{m}\succeq\mathfrak{n}

stands for
(\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}\lor\mathfrak{m}\succ\mathfrak{n})
,
and
\mathfrak{m}\preceq\mathfrak{n}
stands for
\mathfrak{n}\succeq\mathfrak{m}
.
We define an operation
k\otimes\mathfrak{m}

by the recursive equations
0\otimes\mathfrak{m}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[\,]

and
(1+k)\otimes\mathfrak{m}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathfrak{m}+k\otimes\mathfrak{m}
.
The relation
\mathfrak{m}\bm{:}\succ\bm{:}\mathfrak{n}
,
called the
pointwise multiset order
,
is defined to hold if

\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{n}
can be written as of the forms

\mathfrak{m}=[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]

and

\mathfrak{n}=[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]

in such a way that
x_{i}>y_{i}
for all
i\in 1..n
.
Observe that
if
\mathfrak{m}\bm{:}\succ\bm{:}\mathfrak{n}

then for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have that
\mathfrak{m}\succeq k\otimes\mathfrak{n}
.
Another easy-to-check property is that
if
\mathfrak{m}\bm{:}\succ\bm{:}\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}
is non-empty
then
\mathfrak{m}\succ\mathfrak{n}
.

A first frustrated attempt

As mentioned in the introduction,
Turing’s measure, given by

\mathcal{T}(M)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[d\ ||\ \text{$R$ is a redex occurrence of degree $d$ in $M$}]
,
decreases when contracting the rightmost redex of
highest degree.
Our goal is to mend the
\mathcal{T}
-measure in such a way
that contracting
any
redex decreases the measure.
The difficulty is that a redex of degree
d
may copy redexes of
a higher or equal degree
d^{\prime}\geq d
.
So one can wonder: whenever a redex
R
of degree
d

makes
n
copies of a redex
S
of degree
d^{\prime}\geq d
,
in what sense can the copies of
S
be considered “smaller”
than
S
?
To address this, we generalize the
\mathcal{T}
-measure
to a family of measures

\mathcal{T}_{D}(M)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[(d,\mathcal{T}_{d-1}(M))\ ||\ \text{$R$ is a redex occurrence of degree $d\leq D$ in $M$}]

indexed by a degree
D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Note that
\mathcal{T}_{0}(M)
is the empty multiset because
there are no redexes of degree
0
.

Let us try to argue that if
d\leq D
and

M\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}N

then
\mathcal{T}_{D}(M)\succ\mathcal{T}_{D}(N)
.
Here
M\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}N
means that
M\to_{\beta}N

by contracting a redex of degree
d
.
Suppose that the contraction
of the redex
R:M\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}N

copies a redex
S
of degree
d^{\prime}
,
where we assume that
d<d^{\prime}\leq D
,
producing
n
copies
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
.
Note that the contribution of
S
to the multiset
is
(d^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}-1}(M))
,
and the contribution of each
S_{i}
is
(d^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}-1}(N))
.
By induction on
D
, we could inductively argue that

\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}-1}(M)\succ\mathcal{T}_{d^{\prime}-1}(N)
,
since
d^{\prime}-1<d^{\prime}\leq D
.
So far the property would seem to hold.

The problem with this proposal is that
a redex
R
of degree
d
may still
make copies of redexes of degree
exactly

d
,
whose contribution does not necessarily decrease
2
2

2

For example,
in
M=(\lambda x^{0}.\,y^{0\to 0\to 0}\,x^{0}\,x^{0})\,((\lambda z^{0}.\,z^{0})\,w^{0})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{1}_{\beta}}y^{0\to 0\to 0}\,((\lambda z^{0}.\,z^{0})\,w^{0})\,((\lambda z^{0}.\,z^{0})\,w^{0})=N

the measure does not decrease,
as
\mathcal{T}_{1}(M)=[(1,[]),(1,[])]=\mathcal{T}_{1}(N)
.

.

A second frustrated attempt

The difficulty is to deal with the situation in which
a redex
R
of degree
d
makes
n
copies of a redex
S

of the same degree
d
.
A key observation is that a reduction sequence
M\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\beta}N

must be a
development
3
3

3

Recall that a development of a set of
redexes
X
is a reduction sequence
M\to_{\beta}^{*}N

in which each step contracts a
residual
of a redex in
X
.
The residuals of a redex
S:t\to_{\beta}s

after the contraction of a redex
R:t\to_{\beta}t^{\prime}

are, informally speaking, the “copies” left of
S
in
t^{\prime}
.
For formal definitions see
[
3
, Section 11.2]
.

of the set of redexes of degree
d
.
This is because contracting a redex of degree
d
can only create redexes of
degree strictly less than
d
,
so any redex of degree
d
that remains after one
\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}
-step
must be a
residual
of a
preexisting redex.
This motivates our second attempt to define a measure,
consisting of two families of measures
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D}(-)
and

\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{D}(-)
,
indexed by
D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and defined mutually recursively:

\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D}(M)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[(d,\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d}(M))\ ||\ \text{$R$ is a $\beta$-redex occurrence of degree $d\leq D$ in $M$}]

\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{D}(M)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D-1}(M^{\prime})\ ||\ \rho:M\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\beta}M^{\prime}]

Note that there are no redexes of degree
0
,
so
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D}(M)
may not depend on
\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{0}(M)
.
In fact,
\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{D}(M)
is defined only for
D\geq 1
.
The recursive definition is well-founded because

\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D}(M)
may depend on

\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{1}(M),\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{D}(M)

which in turn may only depend on
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq d}(M^{\prime})

for
d<D
.
The multiplicity of
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D-1}(M^{\prime})

in the multiset
\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{D}(M)

is given by the number of reduction sequences that contract only
redexes of degree
D
, that is,
the number of different paths
M\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}M^{\prime}
.
One important point is that, for the measure
\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{D}(t)

to be well defined,
one needs to argue that the number of paths
M\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}M^{\prime}
is finite.
Since
M\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}M^{\prime}
is a development,
this is a consequence of the
finite developments
(FD)
property for orthogonal HRSs
[
31
, Theorem 11.5.11]
.
4
4

4

Note that
FD only ensures that developments are finite. To see that
the set
\{\rho\ |\ M\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}M^{\prime}\}
is finite, one should resort to
König’s lemma, together with the fact that the STLC is finitely branching.
For a constructive proof, one can use a computable decreasing measure,
such as in de Vrijer’s proof of FD
[
9
]
.

Let us try to argue that if
d\leq D
and

M\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}N

then
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D}(M)\succ\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D}(N)
.
On the first hand,
if a redex
R:M\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}N

of degree
d
copies a redex
S
of
exactly
the same
degree
d
making
n
copies
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
,
the contribution of
S
to the multiset is
(d,\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d}(M))
,
whereas each
S_{i}
contributes
(d,\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d}(N))
,
and we can argue that

\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d}(M)\succ\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d}(N)
,
because we can injectively map each reduction sequence

\rho:N\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\beta}N^{\prime}

to the reduction sequence

R\rho:M\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}N\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\beta}N^{\prime}
,
where
R\rho
denotes the composition of
R
and
\rho
.
Furthermore, there is an empty reduction sequence
M\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\beta}M

contributing an element
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq d-1}(M)
to

\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d}(M)
but not to
\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d}(N)
.

On the other hand,
if the contraction of a redex
R:M\xrightarrow{d}_{\beta}N

of degree
d
copies a redex
S
of
strictly
greater
degree
d^{\prime}>d
making
n
copies
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
,
the weight of
S
is
(d^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d^{\prime}}(M))

and the weight of each
S_{i}
is
(d^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d^{\prime}}(N))
,
and we would need to show that

\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d^{\prime}}(M)\succ\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{d^{\prime}}(N)
.
One way to do so would be
to map each reduction sequence
\rho:N\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\beta}N^{\prime}

to a reduction sequence
\sigma:M\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\beta}M^{\prime}

such that
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq d^{\prime}-1}(M^{\prime})\succ\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq d^{\prime}-1}(N^{\prime})
.
However, there does not seem to be a way to rule out
the possibility that
\sigma
might erase
R
and that

M^{\prime}=N^{\prime}
, which would yield

\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq d^{\prime}-1}(M^{\prime})=\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq d^{\prime}-1}(N^{\prime})
,
rather than a strict inequality.
The root of the problem seems again to be
erasure
.

Definition of the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure

The
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure is based on the ideas described above,
but considering reduction in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus rather than in
the STLC, to ensure that there is no erasure.
Informally, the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure is defined by means of the two
following equations.
These equations are exactly as the ones
defining
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\leq D}(-)
and
\mathcal{R}^{\beta}_{D}(-)
above,
with the only difference that they deal
with
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-terms and
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-reduction
rather than with pure
\lambda
-terms and
\to_{\beta}
-reduction:

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[(d,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t))\ ||\ \text{$R$ is a $\mathbf{m}$-redex occurrence of degree $d\leq D$ in $t$}]

\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{D}(t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D-1}(t^{\prime})\ ||\ \rho:t\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}]

To be able to reason about these measures inductively, it will be convenient
to define an auxiliary measure
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)

as the multiset of elements of the form
(d,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0}))

for each
\mathbf{m}
-redex occurrence of degree
exactly

d
in
t
.
This auxiliary measure takes two arguments
t_{0}
and
t
,
and it is defined by structural recursion on the second argument (
t
),
while the first argument (
t_{0}
) is used to keep track of the original
term.
Note that, with this auxiliary definition,
we can write
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)
as the sum

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t,t)+\ldots+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{D}(t,t)
.

To define the measure formally, we start by precisely defining the codomain of the measure.

Definition 23

(Codomain of the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure)
.

For each
d\geq 0
, we define a set
\mathbb{T}_{d}
,
and for
d\geq 1
we define a set
\mathbb{R}_{d}
,
mutually recursively:

\mathbb{T}_{d}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbb{M}(\{(i,b)\ |\ 1\leq i\leq d,\ b\in\mathbb{R}_{i}\})

\mathbb{R}_{d}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{T}_{d-1})

The sets
\mathbb{T}_{d}
and
\mathbb{R}_{d}

are partially ordered by the induced multiset ordering
on their elements.
Tuples
(i,b)
are ordered with the lexicographic order,
that is,
(i,b)>(i^{\prime},b^{\prime})
if and only if

i>i^{\prime}\lor(i=i^{\prime}\land b\succ b^{\prime})
.
Note that
\mathbb{T}_{0}=\{[\,]\}

and that if
d\leq d^{\prime}
then
\mathbb{T}_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{d^{\prime}}

and
\mathbb{R}_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{d^{\prime}}
.
Moreover,
(\mathbb{T}_{d},\succ)
and
(\mathbb{R}_{d},\succ)
are well-founded
partial orders by Thm.
22
.

Given typable
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-terms
t_{0}
,
t
,
and
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\in\mathbb{T}_{d}

and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t)\in\mathbb{T}_{d}
,
and if
d>0
we define
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)\in\mathbb{R}_{d}
,
by induction on
d
as follows.
Note that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)
is defined by a nested
induction on
t
,
and it is also defined on memories (
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},\mathtt{L})
):

Definition 24

(The measures
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(-,-)
,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(-)
, and
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(-)
)
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}c@{\,}l}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},x)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&[\,]\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},\lambda x.\,s)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},s)\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},s\,u)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\begin{cases}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},s^{\prime})+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},\mathtt{L})+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},u)+[(d,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0}))]\\
\hfill\text{if $s=(\lambda x.\,s^{\prime})\mathtt{L}$ and it is of degree $d$}\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},s)+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},u)\hfill\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},s)+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},u)\\
\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},\Box)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&[\,]\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},\mathtt{L})+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{i}(t,t)\\
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(t^{\prime})\ ||\ \rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}]\end{array}

Moreover, the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure
itself is defined for
\lambda
-terms
as follows:

Definition 25
.

If
M
is a typable
\lambda
-term,

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}(M)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(M)

where
D:=\mathsf{maxdeg}(M)
.

When we write
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(M)
, we implicitly regard

M
as a
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-term without any memorized terms.

From a higher-level perspective,
the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)
measure defined above
is the multiset of pairs of the form
(d,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0}))

for each redex of degree
d
in
t
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)

is the multiset of pairs of the form

(d,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t))

for each redex of degree
d\leq D
in
t
.
In particular,
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{0}(t_{0},t)
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t)

are empty multisets, because there are no redexes of degree
0
.
Two easy remarks are that

D\leq D^{\prime}
implies
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D^{\prime}}(t)
,
and that

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t\mathtt{L})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},\mathtt{L})
.

Remark 26
.

As mentioned in the preceding discussion, one important point is that
for
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(-)
to be well-defined we need to argue that the set

\{\rho\ |\ \exists t^{\prime}.\,\,\rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\}
is finite.
This is a consequence of Coro.
19
.

Example 27
.

Let

\Delta:=\lambda x^{0\to 0}.\,x^{0\to 0}(x^{0\to 0}z^{0})

and

W:=\lambda y^{0}.\,w^{0}

and consider the diagram:

\textstyle{t_{2}=w\bm{\{}Wz\bm{\}}\bm{\{}W\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{t_{0}=\Delta\,W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2}
\textstyle{t_{1}=(W(Wz))\bm{\{}W\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{t_{4}=w\bm{\{}w\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}\bm{\}}\bm{\{}W\bm{\}}}
\textstyle{t_{3}=(W(w\bm{\{}z\bm{\}}))\bm{\{}W\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}

Then

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{1})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{2})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{3})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{4})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 1}(t_{4})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{4})=[\,]
, and:

\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{0})=[(2,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{2}(t_{0}))]&\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{2}(t_{0})=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 1}(t_{0}),\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 1}(t_{1})]\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{1})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 1}(t_{1})=[(1,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t_{1})),(1,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t_{1}))]&\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t_{1})=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{1}),\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{2}),\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{3}),\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{4})]\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{2})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 1}(t_{2})=[(1,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t_{2}))]&\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t_{2})=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{2}),\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{4})]\\
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{3})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 1}(t_{3})=[(1,\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t_{3}))]&\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(t_{3})=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{3}),\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 0}(t_{4})]\end{array}

In particular,

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{0})\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{1})\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{2})\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{4})

and

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{1})\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{3})\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq 2}(t_{4})
.

The
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure is decreasing

Lastly, we show the main theorem of this section, stating that
if
M\to_{\beta}N
then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}(M)\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}(N)
.
This theorem is based on three technical results, that we call

high/increase
,
low/decrease
, and
forget/decrease
:

1.

High/increase
(Prop.
29
) establishes
—perhaps confusingly—
that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(-)
(non-strictly)
increases
if one contracts a redex of
higher degree
D>d
. More precisely, if
0\leq d<D
and
t\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t)\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t^{\prime})
.
Note that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t)
only looks at redexes of degree
i\leq d
,
and contracting a redex of degree
D>d

cannot erase
a redex of
any degree
i\leq d
, because the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus is non-erasing.
Contracting a redex of degree
D
can, at most, replicate redexes of degree
i
.
This property is needed for a technical reason to prove the low/decrease
property, and it relies crucially on the commutation result of the previous
section (Prop.
18
).

2.

Low/decrease
(Prop.
30
) establishes
that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(-)

strictly
decreases if one contracts a redex of
lower degree
d<D
. More precisely, if
1\leq d\leq D
and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t^{\prime})
.
This is the core of the argument, and the most technically difficult part
to prove. It relies crucially on the lifting property of the previous
section (Prop.
20
).

3.

Forget/decrease
(Prop.
31
) establishes
that forgetful reduction (non-strictly) decreases the measure.
More precisely, if
t\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}
then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t)\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t^{\prime})
.
This property is used as a final step in the main theorem,
and it relies crucially on postponement of forgetful reduction,
as studied in the previous section (Prop.
21
).

Below we sketch the proofs of these three properties.

See Prop.
65
, Prop.
68
,
and Prop.
69
in the appendix for detailed proofs.

Let us first mention a straightforward lemma.

Lemma 28

(Measure of a substitution)
.

1.

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t[x:=s])
.

2.

If
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
then

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t[x:=s])=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)+k\otimes\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},s)

for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
.

See Lem.
64

and Lem.
67
in the appendix for
details.

∎

Proposition 29

(High/increase)
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then the following hold:

1.

If
1\leq d<D

and
t\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)\preceq\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime})
.

2.

If
0\leq d<D

and
t_{0}\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{0}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},t)
.

3.

If
0\leq d<D

and
t_{0}\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{0}

and
t\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime})
.

4.

If
0\leq d<D

and
t\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t)\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The four items are proved simultaneously by induction on
d
,
where item
1
resorts to the IH,
and the following items may resort to the previous items
without decreasing
d
. Items
2
and
3

proceed by a nested induction on
t
. Most cases are straightforward.

One interesting situation occurs in item
3
when

t=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u

is the redex of degree
D
contracted by the step
t\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.
Then we resort to the first part of Lem.
28
.

Another interesting part of the proof is item
1
.
Let
1\leq d<D
and
t\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

and let us show that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)\preceq\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime})
.
Indeed, let

X:=\{\rho\ |\ (\exists s)\ \rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s\}

and

Y:=\{\sigma\ |\ (\exists s^{\prime})\ \sigma:t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}\}
,
and let
R:t\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.
Using Prop.
18
,
we can define an injective function
\varphi:X\to Y

by
\varphi(\rho):=\rho/R
.
Note that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\rho)^{\mathsf{tgt}})

holds for every
\rho\in X
using item
4
of the IH (noting that
1\leq d-1<D
holds because
1\leq d<D
),
resorting to the IH as many times as the length of the reduction

s\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}_{\rho}
.
To conclude the proof, let
Z=Y\setminus\varphi(X)
. Then:

\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in X]\preceq^{\text{($\star$)}}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\rho)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in X]=^{\text{($\star\star$)}}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in\varphi(X)]

\hphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)}\preceq[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in\varphi(X)]+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Z]=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]=\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime})

To justify the step marked with (
\star
),
note that

[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in X]=\sum_{\rho\in X}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})]\preceq\sum_{\rho\in X}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\rho)^{\mathsf{tgt}})]=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\rho)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in X]

because
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\preceq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\rho)^{\mathsf{tgt}})
,
as we have already claimed.
To justify the step marked with (
\star\star
),
note that
\varphi
is injective.
∎

Proposition 30

(Low/decrease)
.

Let
D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then the following hold:

1.

If
1\leq d\leq j\leq D

and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)\succ\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime})
.

2.

If
1\leq d\leq j\leq D

and
t_{0}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{0}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t_{0},t)\bm{:}\succ\bm{:}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime}_{0},t)
.

3.

If
1\leq d\leq D

and
t_{0}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{0}

and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
,
then for all
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{T}_{d-1}

we have

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime})+\mathfrak{m}
.

4.

If
1\leq d<j\leq D

and
t_{0}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{0}

and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t_{0},t)\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime})
.

5.

If
1\leq d\leq D

and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The five items are proved simultaneously by induction on
D
,
where item
1
resorts to the IH,
and the following items may resort to the previous items
without decreasing
d
. Items
2
–
4

proceed by a nested induction on
t
.
We mention some of the interesting parts of the proof.

For item
1
,
let
1\leq d\leq j\leq D

and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

and let us show that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)\succ\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime})
.
Let
X:=\{\rho\ |\ (\exists{s})\ \rho:t\xrightarrow{j}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s\}

and

Y:=\{\sigma\ |\ (\exists{s^{\prime}})\ \sigma:t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{j}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}\}
,
and consider two subcases:

•

If
d=j
,
let
R:t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
,
define an injective function
\varphi:Y\to X

by
\varphi(\sigma)=R\,\sigma
,
let
Z=X\setminus\varphi(Y)
, and note that:

\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in\varphi(Y)]+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in Z]

\hphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)}=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(R\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in Z]

since
\varphi
is injective

\hphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)}=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in Z]=\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime})+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in Z]

To conclude that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)\succ\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime})
,
note that
Z
is non-empty because it contains
the empty reduction
\epsilon:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t
.

•

If
d<j
,
we construct a function
\varphi:Y\to X
as follows.
By Prop.
20
,
for each reduction
\sigma:t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{j}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}

there exist
s_{\sigma},u_{\sigma}
,
and reductions
\varphi(\sigma):t\xrightarrow{j}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{\sigma}

and
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{j}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{\sigma}

and
s_{\sigma}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{\sigma}
.
Note that for every
\sigma\in Y

we have

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(s_{\sigma})\succ^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(u_{\sigma})\succeq^{\ddagger}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(s^{\prime})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})

where
\dagger
holds by item
5
of the IH observing that
1\leq d\leq j-1<D
because
d<j\leq D
,
and
\ddagger
holds by high/increase (Prop.
29
)
observing that
0\leq j-1<j
.
To conclude the proof,
let
Z=X\setminus\varphi(Y)
, and note that:

\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in\varphi(Y)]+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in Z]

\hphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)}=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in Z]

\hphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t)}\succeq[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]\succ^{(\star)}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]=\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime})

For the step marked with (
\star
),
note that

[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]\bm{:}\succ\bm{:}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]

because

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq j-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})

holds by the claim above
where, moreover,
Y
is non-empty because it contains
the empty reduction
\epsilon:t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{j}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.

Another interesting situation occurs in item
3
,
when
t=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u

is the redex of degree
d
contracted by the step
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.
The step is of the form

t=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s[x:=u]\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}=t^{\prime}
.
Note that
u
is not an abstraction of degree
d
,
because it is the argument of an abstraction of degree
d
.
So by Lem.
28
there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

such that

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},s[x:=u])=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},s)+k\otimes\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},u)
.
The crucial observation is that

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},u)\succeq(1+k)\otimes\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},u)
,
which is because
by item
2

we have that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},u)\bm{:}\succ\bm{:}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},u)
.

Finally, for item
5
,
let
1\leq d\leq D
and
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

and let us show that
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t^{\prime})
.
Indeed:

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{D}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{i}(t,t)\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t,t)+\sum_{j=d+1}^{D}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t,t)

\hphantom{\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)}\succ\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(t^{\prime})+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime})+\sum_{j=d+1}^{D}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t,t)

by item
3
,
taking
\mathfrak{m}:=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(t^{\prime})

\hphantom{\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t)}\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(t^{\prime})+\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime})+\sum_{j=d+1}^{D}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{j}(t^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(t^{\prime})

by item
4
.
∎

Proposition 31

(Forget/decrease)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then the following hold:

1.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)\succeq\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime})
.

2.

If
t_{0}\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}_{0}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},t)
.

3.

If
t_{0}\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}_{0}

and
t\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t_{0},t)\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime}_{0},t^{\prime})
.

4.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}

then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t)\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d}(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The four items are proved simultaneously by induction on
D
,
where item
1
resorts to the IH,
and the following items may resort to the previous items
without decreasing
d
. Items
2
and
3

proceed by a nested induction on
t
.

The interesting part is item
1
,
so let
t\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}
and let us show
that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)\succeq\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime})
.
Let
X:=\{\rho\ |\ (\exists{s})\ \rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s\}

and
Y:=\{\sigma\ |\ (\exists{s^{\prime}})\ \sigma:t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}\}
.
Define an injective function
\varphi:Y\to X

by
\varphi(\sigma):=\sigma^{\curvearrowleft}R
,
resorting to Prop.
21
,
where
\sigma^{\curvearrowleft}R:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{\sigma}
.
and
s_{\sigma}\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}s^{\prime}
.
Note that for every
\sigma\in Y
we have

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(s_{\sigma})\succeq^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(s^{\prime})=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})
,
where
\dagger
holds by item
4

of the IH, observing that
d-1<d
.
To conclude the proof, let
Z=X\setminus\varphi(Y)
, and note that:

\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in\varphi(Y)]+[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in Z]\succeq[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \rho\in\varphi(Y)]

\hphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)}=^{(\star)}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]\succeq^{(\star\star)}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]=\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t^{\prime})

For the step marked with (
\star
), note that
\varphi
is injective.
For the step marked with (
\star\star
),
note that

[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]=\sum_{\sigma\in Y}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})]\succeq\sum_{\sigma\in Y}[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})]=[\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})\ ||\ \sigma\in Y]

because

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\varphi(\sigma)^{\mathsf{tgt}})\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq d-1}(\sigma^{\mathsf{tgt}})
,
as we have already justified.
∎

Finally, we prove the main theorem in this section:

Theorem 32
.

Let
M,N
be typable
\lambda
-terms
such that
M\to_{\beta}N
.
Then
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}(M)>\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}(N)
.

Proof.

Let
D=\mathsf{maxdeg}(M)
and
D^{\prime}=\mathsf{maxdeg}(N)
.
Let
M\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
be the step corresponding to
M\to_{\beta}N
.
By Lem.
11
note that
s\mathrel{\rhd}N
.
Then:

\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}(M)=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(M)\succ^{\text{Prop.~\ref{prop:lower_reduction}}}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(s)\succeq^{\text{Prop.~\ref{prop:shrinking_ame}}}\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D}(N)\succeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}_{\leq D^{\prime}}(N)=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}(N)

The last inequality holds because
D\geq D^{\prime}

since, as is well-known, contraction of a
\beta
-redex in the
simply typed
\lambda
-calculus
cannot create a redex of higher degree.
∎

6
Conclusion

We have defined two decreasing measures for the STLC,
the
\mathcal{W}
-measure (Def.
12
)
and the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure (Def.
25
).
These measures are
decreasing (Thm.
15
and Thm.
32
respectively)
and, to the best of our knowledge, they provide two new proofs of strong
normalization for the STLC.
Both measures are defined constructively and by purely syntactic methods,
using the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus as an auxiliary tool.

The problem of finding a “straightforward” decreasing measure for
\beta
-reduction
in the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus is posed as Problem #26
in the TLCA list of open problems
[
5
]
,
and as Problem #19
in the RTA list of open problems
[
11
]
.

One strength of the
\mathcal{W}
-measure is that its codomain is simple:
each term is mapped to a natural number.
One weakness is that the definition of the
\mathcal{W}
-measure relies on
reduction in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus,
and computing the
\mathcal{W}
-measure
is at least as costly as evaluating the
\lambda
-term itself.
Measures based on Gandy’s
[
16
,
