10
]
have similar
characteristics.
One question is whether the values of the
\mathcal{W}
-measure and
measures based on Gandy’s
can be related. It is not immediate to establish a precise
correspondence.

On the other hand, one strength of the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure
is that it shows how to extend
Turing’s measure
\mathcal{T}(-)
so that it decreases when contracting

any
redex.
The proof is based on a delicate analysis of how contracting
a redex of degree
d
may create and copy redexes of degree
d^{\prime}
,
depending on whether
d<d^{\prime}
, or
d=d^{\prime}
, or
d>d^{\prime}
.
We hope that this may provide novel insights on why the STLC is SN.
The codomain of the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure is not so simple,
as the
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure maps each term to a structure of nested multisets.
Yet, it is “reasonably simple”:
the fact that the partial orders
\mathbb{T}_{d}
and
\mathbb{R}_{d}

are well-founded only relies on the ordinary multiset and lexicographic orderings.
The
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{m}}
-measure is costly to compute; in particular

\mathcal{R}^{\mathbf{m}}_{d}(t)
is defined as a sum over all reductions

\rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
, which may produce a combinatorial explosion.
Another weakness is that our proofs make use of relatively heavy rewriting
machinery, as we have to keep explicit track of witnesses
(
e.g.
in Section
4
).

Besides the techniques mentioned in the introduction,
other proofs of SN of the STLC can be found in the literature.
For example, David
[
7
]
gives a purely syntactic proof of
SN relying on the standardization theorem;
Loader
[
23
]
, as well as Joachimski and Matthes
[
18
]
,
give combinatorial proofs of SN based on inductive predicates
characterizing strongly normalizing terms.
As far as we know, the only proofs that explicitly construct
decreasing measures are those based on Gandy’s.

The idea of keeping “leftover garbage”
can be traced back to at least
the works of Nederpelt
[
21
]
and
Klop
[
20
]
,
who studied non-erasing variants of (possibly) erasing rewriting systems,
in order to relate weak and strong normalization.
Many variations of these ideas have been explored in the past,
such as in de Groote’s notion of
\beta_{S}
reduction
[
8
]

or Neergaard and Sørensen calculus with memory
[
25
]
.
Instead of using the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus, it is possible that other
non-erasing systems may be used.
For instance, Gandy
[
16
]
translates
\lambda
-terms to the
terms of
\lambda{I}
-calculus to avoid erasing arguments.

The definition of reduction in the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus, which allows
arbitrary memory in between the abstraction and the application,
is inspired by Accattoli and Kesner’s work on calculi with
explicit substitutions “at a distance”
[
1
]
.
This mechanism can be traced back, again, to at least the work
of Nederpelt
[
21
]
.

The definition of the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus as a means to obtain an

increasing
measure was inspired by the fact that,
in explicit substitution calculi without erasure,
labeled reduction (in the sense of Lévy labels
[
22
]
)
increases the sum of the sizes of all the labels in the term
[
2
]
.

Appendix A
Technical appendix

A.1
Proofs of Section
2
— The
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus

In this section we give detailed proofs of the results
about the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus stated in Section
2
.

Remark 33
.

t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
if and only if

t
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

Lemma 34

(Substitution lemma)
.

Let
\Gamma,x:A\vdash t:B

and
\Gamma\vdash s:A
.
Then
\Gamma\vdash t[x:=s]:B
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
t
.
∎

Proposition 35

(Subject reduction)
.

Let
\Gamma\vdash t:A
and
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
.
Then
\Gamma\vdash s:A
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the derivation
of the step
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
, resorting to Lem.
34

for the base case, when there is a
\mathbf{m}
-reduction step at
the root.
∎

A.1.1
Confluence of the
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus

Proposition 36

(Confluence)
.

The
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-calculus is confluent.
That is,
if
t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{2}
and
t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{3}
,
there exists a term
t_{4}
such that

t_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{4}
and
t_{3}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t_{4}
.

Proof.

The proof can be done following standard techniques.
For example, following Tait and Martin-Löf’s technique,
we may define a notion of simultaneous reduction
\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}

that allows to contract many redexes simultaneously,

i.e.
allowing the complete development of any set of redexes on
the starting term.
Then it suffices to show that

\to_{\mathbf{m}}\mathrel{\subseteq}\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}\mathrel{\subseteq}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}

and that
\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}
enjoys the diamond property,

i.e.
that if
t_{1}\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}t_{2}
and
t_{1}\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}t_{3}

there exists a term
t_{4}

such that
t_{2}\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}
and
t_{3}\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}
.
The key lemma is:

t\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\text{ and }s\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}\text{ implies }t[x:=s]\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}[x:=s^{\prime}]

The key diagrams in the proof that
\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{m}}
enjoys the diamond
property are:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,s_{2}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{2})\mathtt{L}_{2}\,s_{2}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{3}[x:=s_{3}]\bm{\{}s_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{4}[x:=s_{4}]\bm{\{}s_{4}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{4}}

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,s_{2}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{2}[x:=s_{2}]\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{3}[x:=s_{3}]\bm{\{}s_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{4}[x:=s_{4}]\bm{\{}s_{4}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{4}}

∎

A.1.2
Simplification of a
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
-term

Definition 37

(Generalization of notions to memories)
.

We generalize some of the notions to memories as follows:

1.

The reduction relation
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
is extended to operate on memories
with the two following inductively defined rules:

1.1

If
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
then
\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t^{\prime}\bm{\}}
.

1.2

If
\mathtt{L}\to_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
then
\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}
.

2.

The max-degree is extended to memories as follows:

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\Box)=0

and

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}})=\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{L}),\mathsf{maxdeg}(t))
.

Lemma 38

(Terms reduce to its simplification)
.

For every term
t
and for all
k\geq 1

we have that
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)
.

Proof.

To prove it by induction,
we generalize the statement to memories,

i.e.

\mathtt{L}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L})
.
We proceed by simultaneous induction on
t
and
\mathtt{L}
:

1.

t=x
:
Immediate,
as

x\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}x=\mathtt{S}_{k}(x)
in zero steps.

2.

t=\lambda x.\,s
:
Then

\lambda s.\,\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\lambda x.\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)=\mathtt{S}_{k}(\lambda x.\,s)

by IH.

3.

t=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u

where
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}

is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
k
:
By IH
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}(\lambda x.\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L})\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)\to_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)]\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)\bm{\}}\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L})=\mathtt{S}_{k}((\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u)=\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)
.

4.

t=s\,u

where
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
k
:
By IH
s\,u\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)=\mathtt{S}_{k}(s\,u)=\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)
.

5.

t=s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}
:
By IH
s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)\bm{\}}=\mathtt{S}_{k}(s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}})=\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)
.

6.

\mathtt{L}=\Box
:
Immediate, as

\Box\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\Box=\mathtt{S}_{k}(\Box)
in zero steps.

7.

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}
:
By IH
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime})\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)\bm{\}}=\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}})
.

∎

Lemma 39

(Substitution of terms of lower type does not create abstractions)
.

If
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}>0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}

and
t
not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction,
then
t[x:=s]
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

Proof.

By induction on
t
:

1.

t=y
:
We claim that
y\neq x
.
Indeed, note that the type of
y
is
\mathsf{type}(t)

but the type of
x
is
\mathsf{type}(s)
.
By contradiction, suppose that
x=y
.
Then
\mathsf{type}(t)=\mathsf{type}(s)

and in particular

0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}>0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}
,
which is impossible.
Then we have that
y\neq x
,
so
t[x:=s]=y[x:=s]=y
,
which is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

2.

t=\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}
:
Impossible, since
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction by hypothesis.

3.

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
:
Then

t[x:=s]=t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s]

is trivially not an abstraction.

4.

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
:
Note that
t_{1}
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
by Rem.
33
.
Furthermore, note that
\mathsf{type}(t_{1})=\mathsf{type}(t)

so in particular

0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{1})}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}>0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}
.
We are under the conditions to apply the IH on
t_{1}
,
hence
t_{1}[x:=s]
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.
To conclude, note that

t[x:=s]=t_{1}[x:=s]\bm{\{}t_{2}[x:=s]\bm{\}}

cannot be a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction by Rem.
33
.

∎

Lemma 40

(Simplification does not create abstractions)
.

If
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}\geq k

and
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)\leq k

and
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction,
then
\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

Proof.

By induction on
t
:

1.

t=x
:
Then
\mathtt{S}_{k}(x)=x
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

2.

t=\lambda x.\,s
:
Impossible, since
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction by hypothesis.

3.

t=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u

where
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}

is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
k
:
We claim that this case is impossible.
Writing the types explicitly, we have that

\mathsf{type}(t)=B
with

0pt{B}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}\geq k
by hypothesis.
Then the type of the function must be of the form

\mathsf{type}(\lambda x.\,s)=A\to B
.
But note that

0pt{\mathsf{type}(\lambda x.\,s)}=0pt{A\to B}>0pt{B}\geq k
.
This means that
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}
cannot be of degree
k
,
contradicting the hypothesis of this case.

4.

t=s\,u

where
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
k
:
Then

\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)=s\,u=\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

5.

t=s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}
:
Note that
\mathsf{type}(s)=\mathsf{type}(t)
,
so in particular
0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}\geq k
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)\leq k

and
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
by Rem.
33
.
We are under the conditions to apply the IH on
s
,
hence
\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.
To conclude, note that

\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)\bm{\}}

cannot be a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction by Rem.
33
.

∎

Lemma 41

(Properties of the max-degree)
.

1.

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t\mathtt{L})=\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(t),\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{L}))

2.

If
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)<k

and
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)<k

and
0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}<k

then
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t[x:=s])<k
.

Proof.

Item
1

is straightforward by induction on
\mathtt{L}
,
since
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}})=\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(t),\mathsf{maxdeg}(s))
.
For item
2
,
we proceed by induction on
t
:

1.

t=y
:
We consider two subcases, depending on whether
y=x
or not.
If
y=x
,
then

\mathsf{maxdeg}(x[x:=s])=\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)<k
.
If
y\neq x
,
then

\mathsf{maxdeg}(y[x:=s])=\mathsf{maxdeg}(y)=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)<k
.

2.

t=\lambda y.\,t^{\prime}
:
By
\alpha
-conversion we assume that
x\neq y
.
Note that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t^{\prime})=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\lambda y.\,t^{\prime})=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)<k
by hypothesis.
Then

\mathsf{maxdeg}((\lambda y.\,t^{\prime})[x:=s])=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\lambda y.\,t^{\prime}[x:=s])=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t^{\prime}[x:=s])<k
by IH.

3.

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
:
Note that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1})\leq\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}\,t_{2})=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)<k

and, similarly,
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{2})<k
.
This means that we can apply the IH to obtain that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s])<k

and
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{2}[x:=s])<k
.
We proceed by case analysis, depending on whether

0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{1})}<k
or
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{1})}\geq k
:

3.1

If
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{1})}<k

then, by the substitution lemma (Lem.
34
),
the terms
t_{1}[x:=s]
and
t_{1}
have the same type.
In particular,

t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s]

cannot be a redex of degree
k
or greater,
since
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{1}[x:=s])}<k
.
As a consequence,
if
t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s]

is a redex, its degree is at most
k-1
.
Hence

\mathsf{maxdeg}((t_{1}\,t_{2})[x:=s])=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s])\leq\max(k-1,\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s]),\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{2}[x:=s]))<k
.

3.2

If
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{1})}\geq k
,
note that
t_{1}
cannot be a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction,
because then
t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
would be a redex of
degree
k
or greater,
but by hypothesis we know that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)<k
.
Note that we are under the conditions of
Lem.
39
,
so we know that
t_{1}[x:=s]
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.
In particular,

t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s]

cannot be a redex.
Hence

\mathsf{maxdeg}((t_{1}\,t_{2})[x:=s])=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s])=\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s]),\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{2}[x:=s]))<k
.

4.

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
:
Note that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1})\leq\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}})=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)<k

and, similarly,

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{2})<k
.
This means that we can apply the IH to obtain that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s])<k

and

\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{2}[x:=s])<k
.
Hence

\mathsf{maxdeg}((t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}})[x:=s])=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s]\bm{\{}t_{2}[x:=s]\bm{\}})=\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{1}[x:=s]),\mathsf{maxdeg}(t_{2}[x:=s]))<k
.

∎

Lemma 42

(Simplification decreases the max-degree)
.

Suppose that
k\geq 1
.
If
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)\leq k

then
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(t))<k
.

Proof.

Let
k\geq 1
be such that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)\leq k
.
We argue that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(t))<k
, that is,
all the redexes in
t
have degree less than
k
.
To prove it by induction,
we generalize the statement to memories,
proving also that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L}))<k
.
We prove the statement simultaneously
by induction on
t
and
\mathtt{L}
:

1.

t=x
:
Then

\mathtt{S}_{k}(x)=x

has no redexes, so
\mathsf{maxdeg}(x)=0<1\leq k
.

2.

t=\lambda x.\,s
:
Note that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\lambda x.\,s)\leq k

so by IH
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))<k
.
Moreover,

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(\lambda x.\,s))=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\lambda x.\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))<k
.

3.

t=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u

where
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}

is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
k
:
Note that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)

because any redex in the subterm
s

is also a redex in the whole term
t
,
so in particular
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq k

and we may apply the IH on
s

to conclude that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))<k
.
Similarly, by IH,
we have that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L}))<k

and
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(u))<k
.

Since
t
is typable,
its type is of the form
\mathsf{type}(t)=B

with

\mathsf{type}((\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L})=A\to B

and
\mathsf{type}(u)=A
.
Note that

0pt{\mathsf{type}(u)}=0pt{A}<0pt{A\to B}=k

since
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}
is of degree
k
by hypothesis
of this case.

To conclude this case, note that:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(t))\\
&=&\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)]\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)\bm{\}}\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L}))\\
&&\hskip 14.22636pt\text{by definition}\\
&\leq&\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)]),\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)),\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(\mathtt{L})))\\
&&\hskip 14.22636pt\text{by Lem.~\ref{lemma:maxdeg_properties}~(\ref{maxdeg_properties:sctx})}\\
&<&\max(k,k,k)\\
&&\hskip 14.22636pt\text{by Lem.~\ref{lemma:maxdeg_properties}~(\ref{maxdeg_properties:substitution}) and the IH }\\
&=&k\end{array}

For the last inequality,
we use the fact that
0pt{\mathsf{type}(u)}<k
.

4.

t=s\,u

where
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
k
:
Note that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)

because any redex in the subterm
s

is also a redex in the whole term
t
,
so in particular
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq k

and we may apply the IH on
s

to conclude that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))<k
.
Similarly, by IH, we have that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(u))<k
.

We proceed by case analysis, depending on whether

0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}<k
or
0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}\geq k
:

4.1

If
0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}<k
,
then by Lem.
38

we know that
s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)

and by subject reduction (Prop.
3
)
we have that
\mathsf{type}(s)=\mathsf{type}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))
.
In particular,
\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)

cannot be a redex of degree
k
or greater,
because
0pt{\mathsf{type}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}<k
.
That is, if
\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)
is a redex,
its degree is at most
k-1
.
Hence we have that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(t))=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u))\leq\max(k-1,\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)),\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)))<k
.

4.2

If
0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}\geq k
,
note that
s
cannot be a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.
Indeed, we know by hypothesis of this case
that
s
is not an abstraction of degree
k
.
Furthermore,
s
cannot be an abstraction of degree
k^{\prime}>k
,
because then
t=s\,u

would be a redex of degree
k^{\prime}>k
,
but then we would have that

k<k^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)\leq k
,
which is a contradiction.
Since
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction,

\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq k
, and
0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}\geq k
,
we are under the conditions to apply
Lem.
40

to conclude that
\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.
This means that

\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)

cannot be a redex.
Hence we have that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(t))=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{k}(u))=\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)),\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)))<k
.

5.

t=s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}
:
Note that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)
,
so in particular
\mathsf{maxdeg}(s)\leq k

and we may apply the IH on
s

to conclude that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s))<k
.
Similarly, by IH, we have that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(u))<k
.
Hence we have that

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(t))=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)\bm{\}})=\max(\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(s)),\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(u)))<k
.

6.

\mathtt{L}=\Box
:
Immediate, as
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\Box)=0<1\leq k
.

7.

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}
:
Similar to case
5
of this lemma.

∎

Proposition 43

(Simplification is normalization)
.

t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)

and
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

Proof.

Let
k
be the max-degree of
t
.
For each
0\leq i\leq k
we define
\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t)

as follows, by induction on
k-i
:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathtt{S}_{>k}(t)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&t\\
\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t)&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathtt{S}_{i+1}(\mathtt{S}_{>i+1}(t))&\text{for each $0\leq i<k$}\\
\end{array}

That is,

\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathtt{S}_{i+1}(\ldots\mathtt{S}_{k-1}(\mathtt{S}_{k}(t)))
.
Note that

\mathtt{S}_{>k}(t)=t

and

\mathtt{S}_{>0}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
.
Let us prove each of the two parts of the statement:

1.

To show that
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
,
note that
for each
1\leq i\leq k

we have that

\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t)\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{i}(\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t))=\mathtt{S}_{>i-1}(t)

by Lem.
38
.
Hence:

t=\mathtt{S}_{>k}(t)\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{>k-1}(t)\ldots\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t)\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{>i-1}(t)\ldots\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}\mathtt{S}_{>0}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)

2.

To show that
\mathtt{S}_{*}(t)
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
we claim that for each
0\leq i\leq k

we have that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t))\leq i
.
We proceed by induction on
k-i
.
In the base case, we have that
i=k
,
so
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{>k}(t))=\mathsf{maxdeg}(t)=k

since
k
is the max-degree of
t
.
For the induction step,
let
k-i>0
, so
0\leq i<k
.
By IH we have that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{>i+1}(t))\leq i+1
.
Then

\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t))=\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{i+1}(\mathtt{S}_{>i+1}(t)))<i+1

by Lem.
42
.
This means that
\mathsf{maxdeg}(\mathtt{S}_{>i}(t))\leq i
, as required.

∎

A.1.3
Forgetful reduction

The forgetful reduction relation is generalized to operate on substitution contexts
so that, for example,

(\Box\bm{\{}x\bm{\}}\bm{\{}y\bm{\}})\mathrel{\rhd}(\Box\bm{\{}y\bm{\}})
.

Lemma 44

(Properties of forgetful reduction)
.

1.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}
then
t\mathtt{L}\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}\mathtt{L}
.

2.

If
\mathtt{L}\mathrel{\rhd}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
then
t\mathtt{L}\mathrel{\rhd}t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

3.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}

then
t[x:=s]\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}[x:=s]
.

4.

If
s\mathrel{\rhd}s^{\prime}

then
t[x:=s]\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}t[x:=s^{\prime}]

(in zero or more steps).

Proof.

•

Items
1

and
2

are straightforward by induction on
\mathtt{L}
.

•

Items
3

and
4

are straightforward by induction on
t
.
For item
4
,
note that when
t=y
with
y\neq x
,
we have that

y[x:=s]=y\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}y=y[x:=s^{\prime}]

in exactly zero steps.
Note also that more that one step of
\mathrel{\rhd}

may be required when
t
is an application or a wrapper.

∎

Lemma 45

(Local commutation of reduction and forgetful reduction)
.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}s
and
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
s^{\prime}

such that
t^{\prime}\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s^{\prime}
and
s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{=}s^{\prime}
,
where
\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}
is the transitive closure of
\mathrel{\rhd}
,
and
\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{=}
is the reflexive closure of
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
.
Graphically:

\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}}

Proof.

By induction on
t
:

1.

t=x
:
Note that this case is impossible,
since there are no steps
x\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.

2.

t=\lambda x.\,t_{1}
:
Since
\lambda x.\,t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
,
we know that
t^{\prime}
must be of the form
t^{\prime}=\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1}

with
t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{1}
.
Note that the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal,
that is,

\lambda x.\,t_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}\lambda x.\,s_{1}=s

with
t_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{1}
.
By IH there exists
s^{\prime}_{1}

such that
t^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s^{\prime}_{1}

and
s_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{=}s^{\prime}_{1}
.
Taking
s^{\prime}:=\lambda x.\,s^{\prime}_{1}
we have:

\textstyle{\lambda x.\,t_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{\lambda x.\,s_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{\lambda x.\,s^{\prime}_{1}}

3.

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
:
We consider three subcases, depending on whether the step

t_{1}\,t_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
step at the root,
internal to
t_{1}
, or internal to
t_{2}
:

3.1

If the
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
step is at the root,
then
t_{1}
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
of the form
t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}

and the step is of the form

t=(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}=t^{\prime}
.
Moreover, since

t=(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\mathrel{\rhd}t^{\prime}
,
we consider three further subcases, depending on whether the
step
t\mathrel{\rhd}s

is internal to
t_{11}
, internal to
\mathtt{L}
,
or internal to
t_{2}
:

3.1.1

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{11}
,
then
s=(\lambda x.\,s_{11})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}

with
t_{11}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{11}
.
Taking

s^{\prime}:=s_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}

we have:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,s_{11})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{s_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}}

For the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step at the bottom,
by
Lem.
44
(
1
)
it suffices to show that

t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\mathrel{\rhd}s_{11}[x:=t_{2}]
.
This is a consequence of
Lem.
44
(
3
).

3.1.2

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
\mathtt{L}
,
then
s=(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\,t_{2}

with
\mathtt{L}\mathrel{\rhd}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.
Taking

s^{\prime}:=t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}

we have:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\,t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}

The
\mathrel{\rhd}
step at the bottom holds
by
Lem.
44
(
2
).

3.1.3

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{2}
,
then
s=(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}\,s^{\prime}_{2}

with
t_{2}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{2}
.
Taking

s^{\prime}:=t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}s^{\prime}_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}

we have:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,t_{11})\mathtt{L}\,s_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{t_{11}[x:=s_{2}]\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}}

For the bottom of the diagram, note that:

t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}t_{11}[x:=s_{2}]

by Lem.
44
(
4
).
Hence

t_{11}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}t_{11}[x:=s_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}t_{11}[x:=s_{2}]\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}
.
Resorting to
Lem.
44
(
1
)
we conclude.

3.2

If the
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
step is internal to
t_{1}
,
the step is of the form

t_{1}\,t_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{1}\,t_{2}

with
t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{1}
.
We consider two further subcases, depending on whether
the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is
internal to
t_{1}
or internal to
t_{2}
:

3.2.1

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{1}
,
then
s=s_{1}\,t_{2}

with
t_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{1}
.
By IH there exists
s^{\prime}_{1}

such that
t^{\prime}_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s^{\prime}_{1}
and
s_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{=}s^{\prime}_{1}
.
Taking
s^{\prime}:=s^{\prime}_{1}\,t_{2}
we have:

\textstyle{t_{1}\,t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces s_{1}\,t_{2}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}_{1}\,t_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}_{1}\,t_{2}}

3.2.2

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{2}
,
then
s=t_{1}\,s_{2}

with
t_{2}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{2}
.
Taking
s^{\prime}:=t^{\prime}_{1}\,s_{2}
we have:

\textstyle{t_{1}\,t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces t_{1}\,s_{2}}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}_{1}\,t_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}_{1}\,s_{2}}

3.3

If the
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
step is internal to
t_{2}
,
the proof is similar to the previous case.

4.

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
:
We consider two subcases, depending on whether the step

t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

is internal to
t_{1}
or internal to
t_{2}
:

4.1

If the
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
step is internal to
t_{1}
,
then
t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}=t^{\prime}

with
t_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{1}
.
We consider three further subcases, depending on whether the
step
t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}s
is at the root of the wrapper,
internal to
t_{1}
, or internal to
t_{2}
:

4.1.1

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is at the root of the wrapper,
then
t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}t_{1}=s
.
Taking
s^{\prime}:=t^{\prime}_{1}
we have:

\textstyle{t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces t_{1}}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}_{1}}

4.1.2

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{1}
,
then
s=s_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
with
t_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{1}
,
and we conclude by IH similarly as for case
3.2.1
.

4.1.3

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{2}
,
then
s=t_{1}\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}
with
t_{2}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{2}
,
and we conclude taking
s^{\prime}:=t^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}

similarly as for case
3.2.2
.

4.2

If the
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
step is internal to
t_{2}
,
then
t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t_{1}\bm{\{}t^{\prime}_{2}\bm{\}}=t^{\prime}

with
t_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{2}
.
We consider three further subcases, depending on whether the step

t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}s
is at the root of the wrapper,
internal to
t_{1}
, or internal to
t_{2}
:

4.2.1

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is at the root of the wrapper,
then
t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}t_{1}=s
.
Taking
s^{\prime}:=t_{1}
we have:

\textstyle{t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces t_{1}}
\textstyle{t_{1}\bm{\{}t^{\prime}_{2}\bm{\}}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}}
\textstyle{t_{1}}

4.2.2

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{1}
,
then
t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}=s

with
t_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{1}
,
and we conclude taking
s^{\prime}:=s_{1}\bm{\{}t^{\prime}_{2}\bm{\}}

similarly as for case
3.2.2
.

4.2.3

If the
\mathrel{\rhd}
step is internal to
t_{2}
,
then
t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathrel{\rhd}t_{1}\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}=s

with
t_{2}\mathrel{\rhd}s_{2}
,
and we conclude by IH similarly as for case
3.2.1
.

∎

Proposition 46

(Forgetful reduction commutes with reduction)
.

If
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
and
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
s^{\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s^{\prime}
and
s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}s^{\prime}
.
Graphically:

\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{*}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}}

Furthermore,
if
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
and
t
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
then
s
is also a normal form.

Proof.

First we claim that
if
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
and
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
s^{\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s^{\prime}
and
s\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{=}s^{\prime}
.
This can be seen by induction on the number of
\mathrel{\rhd}
steps
in a reduction sequence
t\mathrel{\rhd}^{+}s
, resorting to
the local commutation lemma (Lem.
45
).

The main statement of the proposition can be seen by induction
on the number of steps in a reduction sequence
t\to_{\mathbf{m}}^{*}t^{\prime}
,
resorting to the claim.

For the “furthermore” part in the statement,
it suffices to show that
if
t\mathrel{\rhd}s
in one step and
t
is a
\to_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
then
s
is also a normal form.
This is straightforward by induction on
t
.
∎

Lemma 47

(Reduce/forget lemma)
.

Let
M\to_{\beta}N
be a
\beta
-step
and let
M\to_{\mathbf{m}}s
be the corresponding step in
\lambda^{\mathbf{m}}
.
Then
s\mathrel{\rhd}N
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
M
:

1.

M=x
:
Impossible, as there are no steps
x\to_{\beta}N
.

2.

M=\lambda x.\,M_{1}
:
Then the step must be of the form

M=\lambda x.\,M_{1}\to_{\beta}\lambda x.\,N_{1}=N

with
M_{1}\to_{\beta}N_{1}
,
and the corresponding step must be of the form

M=\lambda x.\,M_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}\lambda x.\,s_{1}=s

where
M_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}

is the step corresponding to
M_{1}\to_{\beta}N_{1}
.
By IH
s_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}N_{1}
,
so

s=\lambda x.\,s_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}\lambda x.\,N_{1}=N
.

3.

M=M_{1}\,M_{2}
:
We consider three subcases, depending on whether the step is at the root,
internal to
M_{1}
, or internal to
M_{2}
:

3.1

If the step is at the root,
the step must be of the form

M=(\lambda x.\,M_{11})\,M_{2}\to_{\beta}M_{11}[x:=M_{2}]

with
M_{1}=\lambda x.\,M_{11}
,
and the corresponding step is

M=(\lambda x.\,M_{11})\,M_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}M_{11}[x:=M_{2}]\bm{\{}M_{2}\bm{\}}=s
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}s&=&M_{11}[x:=M_{2}]\bm{\{}M_{2}\bm{\}}\\
&\mathrel{\rhd}&M_{11}[x:=M_{2}]\\
&=&N\end{array}

3.2

If the step is internal to
M_{1}
,
the step must be of the form

M_{1}\,M_{2}\to_{\beta}N_{1}\,M_{2}=N

with
M_{1}\to_{\beta}N_{1}
,
and the corresponding step is

M_{1}\,M_{2}\to_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}\,M_{2}=s

where
M_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}

is the step corresponding to
M_{1}\to_{\mathbf{m}}N_{1}
.
By IH we have that
s_{1}\mathrel{\rhd}N_{1}
,
so

s=s_{1}\,M_{2}\mathrel{\rhd}N_{1}\,M_{2}=N
.

3.3

If the step is internal to
M_{2}
,
the proof is similar to the previous case.

∎

A.2
Proofs of Section
4
— Reduction by degrees

In this section we give detailed proofs of the results
about reduction by degrees stated in Section
2
.

Remark 48
.

t\mathtt{L}
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form
if and only if

t
and
\mathtt{L}
are in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

Definition 49

(Steps and reduction sequences)
.

A step of degree
d
—or just step if clear from the context—–
is formally a
5
-uple
R=(\mathtt{C},x^{A},t,\mathtt{L},s)

where
\mathtt{C}
is an arbitrary context
and
\lambda x^{A}.\,t
is an abstraction of degree
d
.
The source of
R
is

R^{\mathsf{src}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathtt{C}[(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s]

and its target is

R^{\mathsf{tgt}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathtt{C}[t[x:=s]\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}]
.
We write
R:t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s
to mean that
R
is a step
of degree
d
with source
t
and target
s
.

A forgetful step —or just step if clear from the context—–
is formally a triple
R=(\mathtt{C},t,s)

where
\mathtt{C}
is an arbitrary context and
t,s
are terms.
The source of
R
is

R^{\mathsf{src}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathtt{C}[t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}]

and its target is

R^{\mathsf{tgt}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathtt{C}[t]
.
We write
R:t\mathrel{\rhd}s
to mean that
R
is a
forgetful step of degree with source
t
and target
s
.

Steps of degree
d
are generalized to reduction sequences of degree
d

(and, respectively, forgetful reduction sequences),
which are sequences of composable steps of the corresponding kind.
Formally, a reduction sequence is a pair

\rho=((t_{0},\ldots,t_{n}),(R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}))

where
(t_{0},\ldots,t_{n})
is a sequence of
n+1
terms
and
(R_{1},\ldots,R_{n})
is a sequence of
n
steps

R_{i}^{\mathsf{src}}=t_{i-1}
and
R_{i}^{\mathsf{tgt}}=t_{i}

for all
i\in 1..n
.
The notions of source and target are extended to reduction sequences
by declaring
\rho^{\mathsf{src}}=t_{0}
and
\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}=t_{n}
.
We write
\rho:t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s
to mean that
\rho
is
a reduction sequence of degree
d
with source
t
and target
s
.
Similarly,
we write
\rho:t\mathrel{\rhd}^{*}s
to mean that
\rho
is
a forgetful reduction sequence with source
t
and target
s

A step
R
can be implicitly treated as the one-step reduction sequence

((R^{\mathsf{src}},R^{\mathsf{tgt}}),R)
.
If
\rho^{\mathsf{tgt}}=\sigma^{\mathsf{src}}
,
we write
\rho\,\sigma
for their composition,
defined as expected.

Definition 50

(Simultaneous reduction of degree
d
)
.

We define a relation
t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
,
meaning that there is a multi-step of degree
d

from
t
to
t^{\prime}
,
inductively by the following rules:

{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\\
&\,\texttt{{p-var}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!x\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}x\end{array}}{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\\
&\,\texttt{{p-abs}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!\lambda x.\,t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}\end{array}}{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636pts\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}\\
&\,\texttt{{p-app${}_{1}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!t\,s\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\,s^{\prime}\end{array}}

{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{L}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636pts\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{$\lambda x.\,t$ is of degree $d$}\\
&\,\texttt{{p-app${}_{2}$}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,s\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}[x:=s^{\prime}]\bm{\{}s^{\prime}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\end{array}}{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636pts\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}\\
&\,\texttt{{p-wrap}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!t\bm{\{}s\bm{\}}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\bm{\{}s^{\prime}\bm{\}}\end{array}}

{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\\
&\,\texttt{{p-ctx-hole}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!\Box\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\Box\end{array}}{\small\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\!\!\mathtt{L}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\hskip 14.22636ptt\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\\
&\,\texttt{{p-ctx-wrap}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
\!\!\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\bm{\{}t^{\prime}\bm{\}}\end{array}}

If
\mathbf{R}
is the derivation witnessing
a multi-step
t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}t^{\prime}

We say that
\mathbf{R}
is empty if it does not use the rule
p-app
2
.
We write
\mathbf{R}:t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}t^{\prime}
if
\mathbf{R}

uses the rule
p-app
2
at least once.

Remark 51

(Simultaneous reduction of terms with memory)
.

1.

t\mathtt{L}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s

if and only if
s
is of the form
t^{\prime}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}

where
t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

and
\mathtt{L}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

2.

Furthermore, the set of derivations

\mathbf{R}:t\mathtt{L}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}

is in bijective correspondence
with the set of pairs of derivations

\mathbf{R}_{1}:t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

and

\mathbf{R}_{2}:\mathtt{L}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 52

(Properties of simultaneous reduction by degrees)
.

1.

For each step
R:t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

there is a multi-step
\mathtt{sim}(R):t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.

2.

For each multi-step
\mathbf{R}:t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

there is a reduction sequence
\mathtt{red}(\mathbf{R}):t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
.
Moreover, if
\mathbf{R}
is non-empty,
then
\mathtt{red}(\mathbf{R})
contains at least one step.

3.

Reflexivity:
t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t
.

4.

Substitution:
If
t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}
and
s\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}

then
t[x:=s]\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}[x:=s^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

All items are straightforward by induction.
∎

Lemma 53

(Commutation of simultaneous reduction by degrees)
.

Let
d,D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Given a step
R:t_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{2}

and a multi-step
\mathbf{S}:t_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{3}
,
there exists a term
t_{4}
,
a multi-step
\mathbf{S}/R:t_{2}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}

and a multi-step
R/\mathbf{S}:t_{3}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}
.
Graphically:

\textstyle{t_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{t_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{t_{4}}

Furthermore:

1.

If
d\neq D
then
R/\mathbf{S}
is non-empty,
i.e.
R/\mathbf{S}:t_{3}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}t_{4}
.

2.

If
d\neq D
,
the first step of
\mathtt{red}(R/\mathbf{S})

determines the step
R
.
More precisely,
suppose that
\mathtt{red}(R_{1}/\mathbf{S})

and
\mathtt{red}(R_{2}/\mathbf{S})

start with the same step. Then
R_{1}=R_{2}
.

Proof.

We prove a more general version of the statement including memories,

i.e.
we prove that for

R:\mathtt{X}_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{X}_{2}
and
\mathbf{S}:\mathtt{X}_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{X}_{3}
,
there exist
\mathtt{X}_{4}

and
\mathbf{S}/R:\mathtt{X}_{2}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{X}_{4}

and
R/\mathbf{S}:\mathtt{X}_{3}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{X}_{4}
,
where
\mathtt{X}_{1},\mathtt{X}_{2},\mathtt{X}_{3},\mathtt{X}_{4}

stand for either terms or memories.
We proceed by induction on
\mathtt{X}_{1}
:

1.

t_{1}=x
:
Impossible, as there are no reduction steps
x\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{2}
.

2.

t_{1}=\lambda x.\,s_{1}
:
Then

R:t_{1}=\lambda x.\,s_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}\lambda x.\,s_{2}=t_{2}

with
s_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}

and

S
must be derived from the
p-abs
rule,
so

S:t_{1}=\lambda x.\,s_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\lambda x.\,s_{3}=t_{3}

with
s_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{3}
.
By IH we have the diagram on the left, and we can construct the
one on the right:

\textstyle{s_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{s_{4}}

\textstyle{\lambda x.\,s_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{\lambda x.\,s_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{\lambda x.\,s_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\lambda x.\,s_{4}}

Furthermore, if
d\neq D
, using the IH it is easy to show that
R/\mathbf{S}
is non-empty
and that
R/\mathbf{S}
determines
R
.

3.

t_{1}=s_{1}\,u_{1}
:
We consider three subcases, depending on whether

R
is at the root, internal to
s_{1}
, or internal to
u_{1}
:

3.1

If
R
is at the root:
Then
s_{1}
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,

i.e.
of the form
s_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}
,
and
R:t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{1}[x:=u_{1}]\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{1}=t_{2}
.
We consider two further subcases, depending on whether

S
is derived using the
p-app
1
or the
p-app
2
rule:

3.1.1

If
S
is derived using the
p-app
1
rule:
Then by Rem.
51

we have that

S:t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}(\lambda x.\,r_{3})\mathtt{L}_{3}\,u_{3}=t_{3}

where
r_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}

and
\mathtt{L}_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}_{3}

and
u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{3}
.
By Lem.
52
we can construct the following diagram,
using reflexivity and
p-app
2
on the bottom:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{r_{1}[x:=u_{1}]\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,r_{3})\mathtt{L}_{3}\,u_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}}

3.1.2

If
S
is derived using the
p-app
2
rule:
Then note that
d=D

and we have that

S:t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}=t_{3}

where
r_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}

and
\mathtt{L}_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}_{3}

and
u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{3}
.
By Lem.
52
we can construct the following diagram,
using reflexivity on the bottom:

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{r_{1}[x:=u_{1}]\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}}

3.2

If
R
is internal to
s_{1}
:
Then
R:t_{1}=s_{1}\,u_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}\,u_{1}

with
s_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}
.
We consider two further subcases, depending on whether

S
is derived using the
p-app
1
or the
p-app
2
rule:

3.2.1

If
S
is derived using the
p-app
1
rule:
Then
S:t_{1}=s_{1}\,u_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{3}\,u_{3}=t_{3}

with
s_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{3}

and
u_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{3}
.
By IH we have the diagram on the left, and we can construct the
one on the right:

\textstyle{s_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{s_{4}}

\textstyle{s_{1}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{2}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{3}\,u_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{s_{4}\,u_{3}}

3.2.2

If
S
is derived using the
p-app
2
rule:
Then
s_{1}
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
,

i.e.
of the form
s_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}
,
and by Rem.
51

we have that

S:t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}

where
r_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}

and
\mathtt{L}_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}_{3}

and
u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{3}
.
Moreover, since we know

s_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}

we consider two further subcases, depending on whether the step

s_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}

is internal to
r_{1}
or internal to
\mathtt{L}_{1}
.
These subcases are similar; we only give the proof
for the case in which the step is internal to
r_{1}
.
In such case

s_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}(\lambda x.\,r_{2})\mathtt{L}_{1}

with
r_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{2}
.
By IH we have the diagram on the left, and we can construct the
one on the right,
using Lem.
52
.
On the right of the diagram, use
p-app
2
.
On the bottom of the diagram,
note that
u_{3}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{3}
by reflexivity
so
r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{4}[x:=u_{3}]
:

\textstyle{r_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{r_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{r_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{r_{4}}

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,r_{2})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{r_{4}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}}

3.3

If
R
is internal to
u_{1}
:
Then
R:t_{1}=s_{1}\,u_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}\,u_{2}

with
u_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{2}
.
We consider two further subcases, depending on whether

S
is derived using the
p-app
1
or the
p-app
2
rule:

3.3.1

If
S
is derived using the
p-app
1
rule:
Then
S:t_{1}=s_{1}\,u_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{3}\,u_{3}=t_{3}

with
s_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{3}

and
u_{1}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{3}
.
By IH we have the diagram on the left, and we can construct the
one on the right:

\textstyle{u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{u_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{u_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{u_{4}}

\textstyle{s_{1}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{1}\,u_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{3}\,u_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{s_{3}\,u_{4}}

3.3.2

If
S
is derived using the
p-app
2
rule:
Then
s_{1}
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
,

i.e.
of the form
s_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}
,
and by Rem.
51

we have that

S:t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}

where
r_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}

and
\mathtt{L}_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}_{3}

and
u_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u_{3}
.
By IH we have the diagram on the left, and we can construct the
one on the right.
On the right of the diagram, use
p-app
2
.
On the bottom of the diagram,
note that
r_{3}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}
by reflexivity
so
r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}r_{3}[x:=u_{4}]
:

\textstyle{u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{u_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{u_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{u_{4}}

\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{(\lambda x.\,r_{1})\mathtt{L}_{1}\,u_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{r_{3}[x:=u_{3}]\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{r_{3}[x:=u_{4}]\bm{\{}u_{4}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}_{3}}

Furthermore, note that if
d\neq D
,
then the multi-step
R/\mathbf{S}:t_{3}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}

at the bottom of the diagram
must be non-empty.
Indeed,
case
3.1.1
,
uses exactly one occurrence of the
p-app
2
rule
to construct
R/\mathbf{S}
.
Case
3.1.2

is impossible, because in such case
d=D
.
In the remaining cases, the bottom of the diagram is constructed
by resorting to the IH,
which means that
R/\mathbf{S}
is non-empty.
An important observation is that in
case
3.3.2

the argument is not erased,
because it is always kept as a memorized term.

Furthermore, if
d\neq D
,
to see that the first step of
\mathtt{red}(R/\mathbf{S})

determines the step
R
,
consider the first step
T
of
\mathtt{red}(R/\mathbf{S})

and note that it its
\lambda
-abstraction can be uniquely traced back
to the
\lambda
-abstraction of
R
(
i.e.
it has a unique ancestor).
Indeed,
in case
3.1.1

the step at the bottom has
R
as its unique ancestor.
Case
3.1.2

is impossible, because in such case
d=D
.
In the remaining cases, it suffices to resort to the IH.

4.

t_{1}=s_{1}\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}
:
We consider two subcases, depending on whether

R
is internal to
s_{1}
or internal to
u_{1}
:

4.1

If
R
is internal to
s_{1}
:
Then
R:t_{1}=s_{1}\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}=t_{2}
.
Note that
S
must be derived using the
p-wrap
rule,
so
S:t_{1}=s_{1}\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{3}\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}=t_{3}
.
By IH we have the diagram on the left, and we can construct the
one on the right:

\textstyle{s_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{s_{4}}

\textstyle{s_{1}\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{2}\bm{\{}u_{1}\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s_{3}\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{s_{4}\bm{\{}u_{3}\bm{\}}}

4.2

If
R
is internal to
u_{1}
:
Similar to the previous case.

Furthermore, if
d\neq D
,
using the IH it is easy to show that
R/\mathbf{S}
is non-empty
and that
R/\mathbf{S}
determines
R
.

5.

\mathtt{L}_{1}=\Box
:
Impossible, as there are no steps
\Box\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{L}_{2}
.

6.

\mathtt{L}_{1}=\mathtt{L}^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\{}t_{1}\bm{\}}
:
Similar to case
4
.

∎

Proposition 54

(Commutation of reduction by degrees)
.

Let
d,D\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
and
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
commute.
More precisely,
given reduction sequences

\rho:t_{1}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{2}

and
\sigma:t_{1}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{3}
,
there exists a term
t_{4}

and reduction sequences

\sigma/\rho:t_{2}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}

and
\rho/\sigma:t_{3}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}
.
Graphically:

\textstyle{t_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{t_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{t_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{t_{4}}

The reduction sequence
\rho/\sigma

is called the projection of
\rho
after
\sigma

and symmetrically for
\sigma/\rho
.
Furthermore:

1.

If
d\neq D
, then
\rho/\sigma

contains at least as many steps as
\rho
.

2.

If
d\neq D
,
then
\rho/\sigma
determines
\rho
.
More precisely,
if
\rho_{1}/\sigma=\rho_{2}/\sigma

then
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
.

Proof.

Recall that

\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}\,\,\subseteq\,\,\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}\,\,\subseteq\,\,\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}
.
by Lem.
52
.
We prove this in two stages.

First, given a reduction sequence
\rho:t_{1}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{2}

and a multi-step
\mathbf{S}:t_{1}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{3}
,
we claim that there exists a term
t_{4}

and constructing
\rho/\mathbf{S}:t_{3}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}

and
\mathbf{S}/\rho:t_{2}\overset{\!D\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{4}
as follows,
by induction on
\rho
,
resorting to Lem.
53

for the constructions of
R/\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{S}/R
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\epsilon/\mathbf{S}&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\epsilon\\
(R\,\rho^{\prime})/\mathbf{S}&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathtt{red}(R/\mathbf{S})(\rho^{\prime}/(\mathbf{S}/R))\end{array}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbf{S}/\epsilon&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathbf{S}\\
\mathbf{S}/(R\,\rho^{\prime})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&(\mathbf{S}/R)/\rho^{\prime}\end{array}

Recall from Lem.
52

that if
\mathbf{R}:u\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u^{\prime}

is a multi-step,
then
\mathtt{red}(\mathbf{R}):u\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u^{\prime}

denotes a reduction sequence.
The inductive cases correspond to the following diagram:

\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}/R}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbf{S}/R)/\rho^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{red}(R/\mathbf{S})}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}/(\mathbf{S}/R)}

For the general case, we proceed by induction on
\sigma

resorting to the previous construction for the constructions of

\mathtt{sim}(S)/\rho
and
\rho/\mathtt{sim}(S)
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rho/\epsilon&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\rho\\
\rho/(S\,\sigma^{\prime})&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&(\rho/\mathtt{sim}(S))/\sigma^{\prime}\end{array}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}\epsilon/\rho&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\epsilon\\
(S\,\sigma^{\prime})/\rho&\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}&\mathtt{red}(\mathtt{sim}(S)/\rho)\,(\sigma^{\prime}/(\rho/\mathtt{sim}(S))\end{array}

Recall from Lem.
52

that if
R:u\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}u^{\prime}

is a step,
then
\mathtt{sim}(R):u\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}u^{\prime}

denotes a multi-step.
The inductive cases correspond to the following diagram:

\scriptstyle{S}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{red}(\mathtt{sim}(S)/\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\rho/\mathtt{sim}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\prime}/(\rho/\mathtt{sim}(S))}
\scriptstyle{(\rho/\mathtt{sim}(S))/\sigma^{\prime}}

Furthermore, if
d\neq D
,
note that
R/\mathbf{S}:t\overset{\!d\,}{\Longrightarrow}_{\mathbf{m}}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}s

by Lem.
53
,
so
\mathtt{red}(R/\mathbf{S}):t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s

by Lem.
52
.
Then by induction on
\rho
, we can show that

\rho/\mathbf{S}
contains at least as many steps as
\rho
.
Finally, by induction on
\sigma
,
we can show that

\rho/\sigma
contains at least as many steps as
\rho
.

Furthermore, to see that

\rho/\sigma
determines
\rho
,
we proceed in stages:

1.

First,
if
\mathtt{red}(R_{1}/\mathbf{S})

and
\mathtt{red}(R_{2}/\mathbf{S})

start with the same step, then
R_{1}=R_{2}

by Lem.
53
.

2.

Second, we can see that if
\rho_{1}/\mathbf{S}=\rho_{2}/\mathbf{S}

then
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}

by induction on
\rho_{1}
.
Note that if
\rho_{1}/\mathbf{S}=\rho_{2}/\mathbf{S}

then
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are either both empty
or both non-empty,
because if
\rho_{1}=R_{1}\,\rho^{\prime}_{1}

then by definition

\rho_{1}/\mathbf{S}=\mathtt{red}(R_{1}/\mathbf{S})\,(\rho^{\prime}_{1}/(\mathbf{S}/R_{1}))

and Lem.
53

ensures that
R_{1}/\mathbf{S}
is non-empty whenever
d\neq D
,
so
\rho_{2}/\mathbf{S}
is non-empty,
and hence
\rho_{2}
is non-empty.
The base case is immediate.
For the induction step, when
\rho_{1}=R_{1}\,\rho^{\prime}_{1}

and
\rho_{2}=R_{2}\,\rho^{\prime}_{2}

we have that
\rho_{1}/\mathbf{S}=\rho_{2}/\mathbf{S}
,
so by definition

\mathtt{red}(R_{1}/\mathbf{S})\,(\rho^{\prime}_{1}/(\mathbf{S}/R_{1}))=\mathtt{red}(R_{2}/\mathbf{S})\,(\rho^{\prime}_{2}/(\mathbf{S}/R_{2}))
.
As before Lem.
53

ensures that
\mathtt{red}(R_{1}/\mathbf{S})

and
\mathtt{red}(R_{2}/\mathbf{S})
are non-empty,
so they must start with the same step.
Hence by Lem.
53

we have that
R_{1}=R_{2}
.
This in turn implies that

\rho^{\prime}_{1}/(\mathbf{S}/R_{1})=\rho^{\prime}_{2}/(\mathbf{S}/R_{2})
,
so by IH
\rho^{\prime}_{1}=\rho^{\prime}_{2}
.

3.

Finally, by induction on
\sigma

we can see that if
\rho_{1}/\sigma=\rho_{2}/\sigma

then
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
,
resorting to the previous item.

∎

Lemma 55

(A term reduces to its simplification, by degrees)
.

For every term
t
and for all
d\geq 1

we have that
t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same proof as that of Lem.
38
,
noting that whenever a redex is contracted, its degree is exactly
d
.
∎

Lemma 56

(Substitution of
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal forms)
.

1.

If
t
and
s
are not
\mathbf{m}
-abstractions of degree
d
,
then
t[x:=s]
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.

2.

Let
t
and
s
be terms in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form
such that
s
is not an abstraction of degree
d
.
Then
t[x:=s]
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

Proof.

We prove the two items separately:

1.

By induction on
t
:

1.1

t=x
:
Then
t[x:=s]=s
, which is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of
degree
d
by hypothesis.

1.2

t=y\neq x
:
Then
t[x:=s]=y
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

1.3

t=\lambda y.\,t^{\prime}
:
By
\alpha
-conversion
we may assume that
y\notin\{x\}\cup\mathsf{fv}(s)
.
Note that
t
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction but, by hypothesis, it cannot
be of degree
d
.
By the substitution lemma (Lem.
34
)
we have that

\mathsf{type}(t[x:=s])=\mathsf{type}(\lambda y.\,t^{\prime}[x:=s])=\mathsf{type}(\lambda y.\,t^{\prime})
,
so
t[x:=s]=\lambda y.\,t^{\prime}[x:=s]

is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction, but it is not of degree
d
.

1.4

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
:
Then

t[x:=s]=t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s]

is an application,
hence not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction.

1.5

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
:
Since

t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
we have that

t_{1}
is also not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.
By IH,

t_{1}[x:=s]
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
so

t[x:=s]=t_{1}[x:=s]\bm{\{}t_{2}[x:=s]\bm{\}}

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.

2.

By induction
t
:

2.1

t=x
:
Then
t[x:=s]=s
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

2.2

t=y\neq x
:
Then
t[x:=s]=y
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

2.3

t=\lambda y.\,t^{\prime}
:
By
\alpha
-conversion
we may assume that
y\notin\{x\}\cup\mathsf{fv}(s)
.
By IH,
t^{\prime}[x:=s]
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
so
t[x:=s]=\lambda y.\,t^{\prime}[x:=s]

is also in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

2.4

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
:
By IH,
t_{1}[x:=s]
and
t_{2}[x:=s]

are in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
To show that the whole term

t[x:=s]=t_{1}[x:=s]\,t_{2}[x:=s]

is a normal form,
we are only left to show that the term does not have
a
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-redex at the root,

i.e.
that
t_{1}[x:=s]
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
of degree
d
.
Note that
t_{1}
cannot be a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
for otherwise
t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
would be a redex of degree
d
,
but we know by hypothesis that
t
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
Hence by item
1

of this lemma,

t_{1}[x:=s]
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
as required.

2.5

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
:
By IH,
t_{1}[x:=s]
and
t_{2}[x:=s]

are in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
so
t[x:=s]=t_{1}[x:=s]\bm{\{}t_{2}[x:=s]\bm{\}}

is also in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

∎

Lemma 57

(Simplification does not create abstractions, by degrees)
.

If
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
then
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
:

1.

t=x
:
Then
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=x
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.

2.

t=\lambda y.\,s
:
Note that
t
is an abstraction but, by hypothesis, it cannot be
of degree
d
.
By the fact that a term reduces to its simplification (Lem.
38
)
and by the substitution lemma (Lem.
34
)
we know that
\mathsf{type}(\mathtt{S}_{d}(t))
is not of degree
d
,
so in particular it cannot be a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.

3.

t=(\lambda y.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u
,
where
(\lambda y.\,s)\mathtt{L}
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
:
Then

\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)]\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)\bm{\}}\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})
.
To show that this term is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
it suffices to show that
\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)]

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.
Note that the abstraction
\lambda y.\,s

is of type
A\to B

where
\mathsf{type}(s)=B
and
\mathsf{type}(u)=A
.
In particular, since the abstraction
\lambda y.\,s

is of degree
d
, we have that
0pt{A\to B}=d
.
Furthermore,
by the fact that a term reduces to its simplification (Lem.
38
)
and by subject reduction (Prop.
3
),
we know that

0pt{\mathsf{type}(\mathtt{S}_{d}(s))}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}=0pt{B}<d

and

0pt{\mathsf{type}(\mathtt{S}_{d}(u))}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(u)}=0pt{A}<d
.
In particular,
\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)
and
\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)

cannot be
\mathbf{m}
-abstractions of degree
d
.
Finally
by Lem.
56
(
1
)
this means that
\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)]

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
as required.

4.

t=s\,u
,
where
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
:
Then

\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)

is an application, hence not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.

5.

t=s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}
:
Since
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
we know that
s
is also not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.
By IH,

\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
,
so

\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)\bm{\}}

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.

∎

Lemma 58

(The simplification of a term is normal, by degrees)
.

\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

Proof.

By induction on
t
,
generalizing the statement also for memories,

i.e.
showing that
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form:

1.

t=x
:
Then
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=x
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

2.

t=\lambda x.\,s
:
Then
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=\lambda x.\,\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)

is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form
because
\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form by IH.

3.

t=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,u
,
where
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
:
Then

\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)]\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)\bm{\}}\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})
.
Note that, by IH,

\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)
,
\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)
, and
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})

are in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

Since
(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}
is an abstraction of degree
d
,
we know that
0pt{\mathsf{type}(u)}<d
.
Hence
by the fact that a term reduces to its simplification (Lem.
38
)
and by subject reduction (Prop.
3
)
we know that
0pt{\mathsf{type}(\mathtt{S}_{d}(u))}<d
.
In particular,
\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)
is not an abstraction of degree
d
.
This allows us to apply Lem.
56
(
2
)
to conclude that
\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)]

is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
This lets us conclude that

\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)[x:=\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)]\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)\bm{\}}\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})

is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

4.

t=s\,u
,
where
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.
Then
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)\,\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)
,
where by IH we have that
\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)
and
\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)

are in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
and by Lem.
57

we have that

\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
d
.
Hence
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

5.

t=s\bm{\{}u\bm{\}}
:
Then
\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)=\mathtt{S}_{d}(s)\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(u)\bm{\}}

and we conclude by IH.

6.

\mathtt{L}=\Box
:
Immediate, as
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\Box)=\Box
is in
\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

7.

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}\bm{\{}t\bm{\}}
:
Then
\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})=\mathtt{S}_{d}(\mathtt{L})\bm{\{}\mathtt{S}_{d}(t)\bm{\}}

and we conclude by IH.

∎

Lemma 59

(Reduction does not create redexes of higher degree)
.

Let
d\leq D
and suppose that
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s
.

1.

If
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
,
then
s
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.

2.

If
t
is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
then
s
is also in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

Proof.

We prove the two items independently:

1.

By induction on
t
:

1.1

t=x
:
This case is impossible, as there are no reduction steps
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s
.

1.2

t=\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}
:
Note that
t
is a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction, so by IH it cannot be of
degree
D
, that is,
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}\neq D
.
By subject reduction (Prop.
3
)
we have that
0pt{\mathsf{type}(s)}=0pt{\mathsf{type}(t)}\neq D
,
so
s
cannot be a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.

1.3

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
:
We consider three subcases, depending on whether the reduction is
at the root, internal to
t_{1}
, internal to
t_{2}
:

1.3.1

If the reduction is at the root:
Then
t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1})\mathtt{L}
is an abstraction of degree
d
,
and the step is of the form

t=(\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{1}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}=t^{\prime}
.
Note that
\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1}
is an abstraction of degree
d
,
so its type is of the form
A\to B
with

0pt{A\to B}=d
.
The type of the body of the abstraction
is
\mathsf{type}(t^{\prime}_{1})=B
,
so
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t^{\prime}_{1})}=0pt{B}<d\leq D
,
and the type of the argument of the abstraction
is
\mathsf{type}(t_{2})=A
,
so
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{2})}=0pt{A}<d\leq D
.
This means that
t^{\prime}_{1}
and
t_{2}
cannot be

\mathbf{m}
-abstractions of degree
D
.
Hence by Lem.
56
(
1
)
we have that
t^{\prime}_{1}[x:=t_{2}]

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstractions of degree
D
.
From this we conclude that
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime}_{1}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstractions of degree
D
.

1.3.2

If the reduction is internal to
t_{1}
:
Then the step is of the form

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}\,t_{2}=s

with
t_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}
.
Note that
s
is an application,
and hence not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.

1.3.3

If the reduction is internal to
t_{2}
:
Then the step is of the form

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{1}\,s_{2}=s

with
t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}
.
Note that
s
is an application,
and hence not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.

1.4

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
:
We consider two subcases, depending on whether the reduction
is internal to
t_{1}
or internal to
t_{2}
:

1.4.1

If the reduction is internal to
t_{1}
:
Then the step is of the form

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}=s

with
t_{1}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}
.
By hypothesis
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
,
so
t_{1}
is also not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.
By IH
s_{1}
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
,
so we conclude that

s=s_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.

1.4.2

If the reduction is internal to
t_{2}
:
Then the step is of the form

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{1}\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}=s

with
t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{2}
.
By hypothesis
t
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
,
so
t_{1}
is also not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.
Hence
s=t_{1}\bm{\{}s_{2}\bm{\}}

is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.

2.

By induction on
t
:

2.1

t=x
:
This case is impossible,
as there are no reduction steps
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s
.

2.2

t=\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}
:
Straightforward resorting to the IH.

2.3

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
:
We consider three subcases, depending on whether the reduction is
at the root, internal to
t_{1}
, internal to
t_{2}
:

2.3.1

If the reduction is at the root:
Then
t_{1}=(\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1})\mathtt{L}
is an abstraction of degree
d
,
and the step is of the form

t=(\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}_{1}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}=s
.
Note that by hypothesis,
t=(\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1})\mathtt{L}\,t_{2}

is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
which means in particular that
t^{\prime}_{1}
,
\mathtt{L}
and
t_{2}

are in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
Moreover, since
\lambda x.\,t^{\prime}_{1}
is an abstraction of degree
d
,
its type is of the form
A\to B
with

0pt{A\to B}=d
.
Moreover, its argument
t_{2}
is such that

\mathsf{type}(t_{2})=A
,
so
0pt{\mathsf{type}(t_{2})}=0pt{A}<d\leq D
.
In particular,
t_{2}
cannot be an abstraction of degree
D
.
By Lem.
56

this implies that
t^{\prime}_{1}[x:=t_{2}]
is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
Finally, this means that
t^{\prime}_{1}[x:=t_{2}]\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}\mathtt{L}

must also be in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

2.3.2

If the reduction is internal to
t_{1}
:
Then the step is of the form

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s_{1}\,t_{2}=s
.
By hypothesis
t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
so we kwow that
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
must be in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form
and, moreover, that
t_{1}
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.
By IH, we have that
s_{1}
is a
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
Moreover, by item
1

of this lemma, we have that
s_{1}
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction
of degree
D
.
Hence we conclude that
s=s_{1}\,t_{2}

is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

2.3.3

If the reduction is internal to
t_{2}
:
Then the step is of the form

t=t_{1}\,t_{2}\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}t_{1}\,s_{2}=s
.
By hypothesis
t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
so we kwow that
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
must be in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form
and, moreover, that
t_{1}
is not a
\mathbf{m}
-abstraction of degree
D
.
By IH, we have that
s_{2}
is a
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.
Hence we conclude that
s=s_{1}\,t_{2}

is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form.

2.4

t=t_{1}\bm{\{}t_{2}\bm{\}}
:
Straightforward resorting to the IH.

∎

Proposition 60

(Lifting property for lower steps)
.

Let
d<D
and suppose that
t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}
.
Then there exist a term
t^{\prime}
and a term
s^{\prime\prime}

such that
t\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}t^{\prime}

and
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}

and
t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-8.44455pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}
in at least one step.
Graphically:

\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{s^{\prime\prime}}

Proof.

Take
t^{\prime}:=\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)
.
By the fact that a term reduces
to its simplification (Lem.
55
)
we have that
t\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)
.
Appyling commutation (Prop.
18
)
on the reduction sequences

t\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s

and

t\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)
,
we have that
there exists a term
u
such that

s\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u

and

\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$+$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u
in at least one step.
Applying the commutation theorem again,
this time on the reduction sequences

s\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u

and

s\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}

we have that there exists a term
s^{\prime\prime}

such that
u\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}

and
s^{\prime}\xrightarrow{D}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime\prime}
.
The situation is:

\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{s^{\prime\prime}}

By Lem.
58

we know that
\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)
is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
and since
\mathtt{S}_{D}(t)\xrightarrow{d}^{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{{\scriptsize$\ast$}}}_{\mathbf{m}}u
with
d<D
,
by Lem.
59
(
2
)
we have that
u
is in
\xrightarrow{D}_{\mathbf{m}}
-normal form,
so
u=s^{\prime\prime}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 61

(Local postponement of forgetful reduction)
.

If
R:t\mathrel{\rhd}s
is a forgetful step and

S:s\xrightarrow{d}_{\mathbf{m}}s^{\prime}
